#524. Eli Lilly’s Foundayo obesity pill eases sleep apnea in late-stage trial, paving way for approval

Eli Lilly on Monday said its obesity pill Foundayo significantly reduced the severity of obstructive sleep apnea, the most common sleep-related breathing disorder, in patients with obesity, in a late-stage trial.

The drugmaker plans to file by the end of the year for U.S. approval of the daily pill for moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea, or OSA, in adults with obesity or who are overweight. The results make the pill — which rolled out in the U.S. in April — one of several GLP-1 treatments to show promise in treating the condition.

OSA refers to interrupted breathing during sleep due to narrowed or blocked airways, which can cause drops in oxygen and increases in blood pressure that are associated with serious complications, such as hypertension, heart failure and stroke. The condition affects nearly 1 billion adults globally, including 80 million people ages 20 and over in the U.S., according to the National Council on Aging. Most have never been diagnosed, Lilly said in a release.

Lilly's blockbuster weight loss injection Zepbound became the first medication to win U.S. approval for OSA in December 2024. Patients have limited treatment options outside of that drug and many use cumbersome machines that provide positive airway pressure, or PAP, to allow for normal breathing.

"Foundayo is already making a difference for people living with obesity, and with plans to submit these results to the FDA by the end of the year, we could have the opportunity to help people living with OSA," Dr. Thomas Seck, senior vice president of product development of Lilly Cardiometabolic Health, said in a release.

The trial tested Foundayo in two independent studies, the first of which included 363 patients who were unable or unwilling to use PAP therapy. The second study included 349 people who were on PAP therapy for at least three months at the time of screening and continued that treatment during the trial.

Patients in both studies who took Foundayo started at a low dose and increased it gradually at four-week intervals to different maximum dosages.

Researchers specifically examined how much the pill reduced the so-called apnea-hypopnea index, or AHI, which records the number of times per hour a person's breathing shows a restricted or completely blocked airway. The index is used to evaluate the severity of obstructive sleep apnea and the effectiveness of treatments for the condition.

People who took Foundayo and were not using PAP therapy had an average of 21.1 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 4.8 per hour for the placebo, according to the results.

Foundayo also met the trial's second goal in that group, leading to an overall AHI reduction of 43.8% compared with a decrease of 6.3% for the placebo. Lilly said 31.5% of the participants in the Foundayo group met the criteria for remission or improvement to mild OSA.

Meanwhile, patients who were on both Foundayo and PAP therapy had an average of 18.3 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 5.5 per hour for the placebo.

Foundayo led to an overall AHI reduction of around 27.5% in that group, compared with a decrease of 3% for the placebo. The company said 19.6% of the participants on Foundayo and PAP therapy also met the criteria for remission or mild OSA.

Foundayo also reduced what's known as the sleep apnea-specific hypoxic burden — a measure of how deeply and for how long oxygen levels fall during sleep. That metric fell by 55.9% in people not using PAP therapy and 45.8% in those using it.

Lilly said the pill also helped patients lose roughly 11% of their weight across groups at 52 weeks and helped to lower a marker of inflammation.

The company said the safety data and how well patients tolerated Foundayo were generally consistent with previous trials on the pill.

Around 12% of people on Foundayo who were not using PAP therapy discontinued due to side effects, compared to 3.8% with the placebo. Meanwhile, 6.8% of those on both Lilly's pill and PAP discontinued the drug due to side effects, compared with 0.6% in the placebo group.

The most common side effects were gastrointestinal, including nausea, diarrhea, constipation and vomiting, and were generally mild to moderate in severity. That's consistent with other GLP-1 medications.

#521. Google Wanted Spirit Airlines Data. Flight Attendants Found a Way to Opt Out

An agreement reached this week between Google and a union representing an unlikely workforce—flight attendants—could reveal a more permanent answer by creating a process that might reliably separate the data that workers produce from their own personal information. A judge still needs to approve the agreement next week.

The revised deal requires Spirit to hire a third-party data de-identification company, called Tonic AI, to exclude information that could potentially be associated with individual consumers or employees. Tonic will also be charged with ensuring that data, once de-identified, cannot be relinked to specific people. Google has promised to maintain the data in this new, de-identified form.

Many sensitive records will be excluded from the sale, including employee medical records, documents related to employee investigations and labor relations, and information about industry-wide safety management systems. In early November, a trade organization representing airlines chimed in on the sale, arguing that including that information could make employees less willing to come forward to report safety issues through voluntary programs, which are widely regarded as critical to the US aviation industry’s strong safety record.

The proposed agreement also includes an opt-out clause. Google can choose to renege on the sale if the data-cleaning process reduces the assets by more than 8 percent. Google didn’t respond to a request for comment.

Association for Flight Attendants president Sara Nelson tells WIRED she’s as surprised as anyone that flight attendants are at the forefront of AI-related workers’ rights issues, which often focus on employees who, willingly or unwillingly, train their machine replacements.

“It’s very hard to replace a flight attendant—you’d need a high-functioning robot, and that seems to be further off,” she says. “What this shows is that this is an issue even in jobs you think are most protected from AI. This is an issue for everyone.”

She said the union will immediately begin to demand that other airlines include worker-data-protection clauses in their labor agreements. “If you’re not willing to meet these standards for your workers, we’re going to shame you,” she says. The Association for Flight Attendants has begun meeting with state-level politicians to craft legislation that would write worker data protections into law.

Tech leaders have argued that workers understand that their information will train AI models, which will ultimately make their lives better and easier. “You have to think of the upside, the abundance that AI can result in,” one executive told WIRED this month.

Nelson says US workers aren’t buying the upside of AI and that they’re part of the wider electorate that seems to be growing warier of the tech by the day. She says the vision of AI abundance is “a fantasyland of people in boardrooms with beanbags who wear hoodies.”

#514. Alex Karp Said He’s a ‘Progressive.’ OK! Here’s Some Other Stuff He’s Said Recently

When Palantir chief executive Alex Karp sat down with Politico this week, he reportedly used the word “progressive” eight times to describe both his political philosophy and his company.

“I’ve been a Democrat my whole life,” Karp told Politico, which noted that he called the Democratic Party “totally irresponsible” in the distant time of October 2025. “I know more about being a progressive than almost anybody on the planet, and I absolutely believe Palantir—all of our products are essentially progressive.”

This isn’t the first time Karp has called himself a progressive. In 2018, The New York Times reported that Karp described himself as such. In the same article, he claimed to have voted for Hillary Clinton.

Karp has also donated money to Democratic candidates. In 2023, for instance, he donated $360,000 to a joint fundraising committee for Joe Biden and Kamala Harris. The next year, he donated $1 million to MAGA, Inc.

Palantir has a share of supporters from the Democratic party. However, it’s not necessarily clear how the products of Palantir—a company that built the technology that underpins much of the Trump administration's brutal arrest and deportation campaign, sold tools to the US military that can use generative AI to help plan and execute drone strikes, and partnered with the Israeli military to assist in its devastating war on the Gaza strip, among other things—could reasonably be seen as progressive.

It’s instructive, then, to look at what else Karp has said in recent weeks, and how it lines up with his idea of what’s “progressive.”

In his most recent quarterly letter to Palantir shareholders, Karp actually doubled down on his basic premise. In it, he argued that Palantir employees are “the pioneers of a genuine and viable antiwar movement.”

“We are all, in no uncertain terms, living through what John Lewis Gaddis, the professor of naval and military history at Yale, presciently described decades ago as the long peace,” Karp wrote, despite the fact that the US and Iran are currently engaged in direct warfare.

During Palantir’s corresponding earnings call on November 2, Karp was asked to clarify the statements in his letter.

“Our products are built to avoid war, meaning the adversary knows that they will have such pain inflicted upon them that in fact it's not worth going to war,” Karp said. He did not, however, address the relevant possibility that the US is the party that initiates war.

“I grew up as a lefty,” Karp continued. “I don't actually like war. I want to avoid war. I don't support wars.” But he said, “Sometimes they are necessary, and when they are necessary, America and its allies need the very, very, very best tools.”

Karp went on to say that Palantir has both a “moral imperative” and a “mission reason” for having the “somewhat crazy aspiration” of dominating its market. That mission, Karp said, is supporting the war fighter. Conveniently, it’s also very profitable.

“We are delighted that we can support the war fighter even more because we're making so much money,” Karp said.

Karp’s recent comments in Politico could also be seen through the lens of convenience. Democrats will have a majority in both chambers of Congress next year, and since about half of Palantir’s revenue comes from government work, it’s pragmatic to sow goodwill. Palantir has seen continued success, after all, across Democratic and Republican administrations.

However, Karp doesn’t care about cultivating favor with everybody. He pointedly excludes anyone who has criticized Palantir in what he calls “ridiculously stupid and malicious, incredibly hard to understand attacks.” (Karp didn’t name examples, although many critiques of Palantir have come from the American left.)

“What motivates me is helping to make sure we are fighting for you,” Karp said, addressing Palantir investors, “and quite frankly, making sure that the people that made fun of you and made fun of us are suffering.”

#510. Goodyear Tire CEO Mark Stewart steps down as company continues turnaround plan

Goodyear Tire & Rubber Co. announced Monday that CEO Mark Stewart would be stepping down from his role effective immediately.

Board member Roger Wood is taking over as interim CEO and president, Goodyear said.

The leadership change comes as the tire company is in the midst of a multiyear transformation plan.

Stewart was hired in January 2024 to lead the company, with the goal of revitalizing the legacy American business to make it more efficient and profitable.

The company has faced disappointing results thus far, reporting last week third-quarter results that saw net sales down 2.3% year over year and an adjusted net loss of $31 million. Goodyear failed to reach a 10% operating margin by the end of last year, which was a major goal of the turnaround plan.

Goodyear said it formed a special committee of the board earlier this year to conduct a "strategic review of the company's businesses and operations focused on identifying opportunities to strengthen performance and drive shareholder value."

It said Monday it will continue that work by evaluating strategic and operational initiatives that include further optimizing the company's business mix and footprint, improving efficiency and investing in new products and capabilities that support long-term growth.

"Goodyear has made meaningful progress in becoming a more efficient and focused company, and the board believes this is the right moment to transition leadership to accelerate that progress and capture additional opportunity," Goodyear Chair Joe Hinrichs said in a press release.

The company has faced headwinds from tariffs, higher raw material costs and increased competition from China.

Wood has been a member of the company's board of directors since July 2023 and was previously the co-CEO of Tenneco as well as CEO of Dana Holding Corporation.

The company will start a search for a permanent replacement as part of its strategic review.

Shares of Goodyear were down roughly 4% in early trading Monday.

#509. You Can Buy T-Shirts Glorifying Mass Shootings Almost Anywhere Online

On the day he carried out the Columbine High School massacre, 18-year-old shooter Eric Harris wore a white T-shirt with the phrase “Natural Selection” printed on it in black lettering. His fellow assailant, 17-year-old Dylan Klebold, wore a black T-shirt with the word “Wrath” printed in red across the chest.

Ever since, this apparel has been closely linked with mass murder, part of an ever-growing mosaic of niche allusions to deadly attacks on schools, places of worship, government buildings, and social gathering spaces. While you might not think twice about a person wearing one of the shirts Harris and Klebold chose for their assault on their classmates, let alone recognize the reference, there’s an online community that revels in this aesthetic, using it as a means to valorize and motivate nihilistic violence.

According to a report publishing later this week from the Institute for Strategic Dialogue, a research organization that analyzes extremism and online threats, statement shirts associated with such horrific acts are widely available from at least half a dozen internet retailers.

ISD found sellers peddling “Wrath” and “Natural Selection” tees modeled after the Columbine shirts on TikTok Shop, Redbubble, Temu, Etsy, AliExpress, and Walmart. Shirts with the slogan “Humanity Is Overrated”—like the one worn by a teen gunman who killed nine people at his high school in Finland in 2007—turned up on AliExpress, Temu, and the TikTok Shop, according to the report.

In a statement, a representative for TikTok US said: “Discriminatory and offensive products are prohibited from sale on TikTok Shop and will be removed.” A representative for Redbubble said that the marketplace prohibits “content that glorifies or trivializes violence” and that it had removed listings for “Wrath” and “Natural Selection” shirts flagged by WIRED.

A representative for Walmart said that a shirt flagged by WIRED was removed in accordance with its policies for third-party sellers.

None of the other retailers immediately replied to a request for comment.

Katherine Keneally, ISD’s senior director of threat analysis and prevention, tells WIRED that these clothes are promoted by a cohort known as the True Crime Community, or TCC. Not to be confused with fans of podcasts and documentaries about unsolved or unusual crimes, the TCC “is a predominantly online fandom that is rooted in an obsession with mass killers, regardless of the mass killer’s motivation,” Keneally says.

The TCC’s fascination with the lore around specific killers can lead to dissection of details as minor as what they had for breakfast on the morning of their attack, Keneally says. But the statement shirts offer a convenient way to covertly endorse such massacres, with most people outside the in-group none the wiser.

“What we see from attackers is that they are often trying, in many ways, to mirror previous attackers, but putting their own sort of spin on it,” Keneally says. The shirts are used to “signal interest and glorification of the violence, and also can serve as a sign of someone's plans to engage in something, engage in some sort of attack,” she adds. The ISD’s report cites the cases of a 13-year-old arrested in South Africa last month after allegedly stabbing a 6-year-old while wearing a “Wrath” shirt and an 18-year-old accused of fatally shooting two people in a California library in June, who was apprehended wearing a “Natural Selection” tee.

The ISD analysis of the sale of shirts connected to past shootings is part of a planned series by the nonprofit covering various warning signs of future attacks. The idea is to give parents, teachers, and law enforcement more guidance in understanding when a young person may have intent to harm others, particularly because “within the TCC, we are seeing predominantly youth and minors being involved,” Keneally says.

Adolescents aren’t just engaging with this content in “the darker corners of the internet,” Keneally says. “It's happening very openly across mainstream and fringe platforms.” The prevalence of the clothing across an array of online shops is an indication of how the TCC’s extremist messaging flies under the radar.

Meanwhile, as ISD revealed in its research, younger affiliates of this diffuse collective are trading tips on how to disguise the meaning of paraphernalia tied to mass shooter fandom. One screenshot in the report shows a TikTok user asking their followers, “How do y’all convince your parents to buy the natural selection shirt,” with commenters advising that they pretend “Natural Selection” is the name of a movie, a video game, or a brand. One person replied: “My parents don’t know what tcc is so i can buy it without any problems.”

TCC enthusiasts have also made it clear in their product reviews of the T-shirts that they admire perpetrators of deadly gun violence. In their five-star feedback on a “Wrath” shirt, a customer wrote “we love dylan klebold!” Another attached an anime-style cartoon of Eric Harris in the “Natural Selection” shirt on their review of same, noting that they were hiding it from their mom but that it was “so cute” and “good quality too.” A third person ominously noted that they would use their “Wrath” shirt “to become a bit more popular at school.”

Keneally acknowledges that there’s a “fine line” to consider with clothing that may suggest an unhealthy fixation on infamous killers, since even the best-known examples are not offensive or illegal in themselves. Context also matters: The TCC has an affinity for the German industrial band KMFDM because they were a favorite band of the Columbine shooters, but a fan wearing a KMFDM shirt could have no idea of the connection.

While a teen’s purchase of the most flagrant TCC shirt doesn’t help them commit an attack or necessarily indicate the planning of real-world violence, Keneally says, adults should weigh it against other factors. “If they have access to weapons or maybe another student has heard them talk about committing violence, then it can help serve as a warning sign and provide an opportunity for intervention,” she says. And wearing the shirt at all communicates a likely need for mental health support.

As for the retailers that facilitate the sale of this apparel, Keneally believes that if they adequately enforced their established safety policies, much of it would be taken down. The clothes are something that youths seek out to be “a part of” the True Crime Community and fuel this toxic subculture, she says. “It shouldn't be that easy to access.”

Update, Thursday November 12 at 1pm: The story was updated to include a statement from Walmart, which has removed the shirts mentioned in the story.

The Series 11 earned a Mashable Choice Award for its big battery life upgrade. Compared to the Series 10 from 2024, it lasts up to six hours longer for a total of 24 hours. The brand-new Apple Watch Series 12 has the same battery life, though it charges a bit faster.

The Series 11 doesn't have the same upgraded optical and electrical sensors as the Series 12, and its processor is a generation behind. It remains a good option for folks who aren't interested in the most cutting-edge health tracking tools — and those who are a little sketched out by Audio Intelligence.

#505. Honda to invest $3 billion in new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio

MARYSVILLE, Ohio — Honda Motor plans to invest $3 billion to build a new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio, the automaker announced Monday.

Honda said the new plant, which will be near its other facilities in Marysville, Ohio, is expected to begin production in 2029, have a capacity for 240,000 vehicles annually and create more than 2,000 jobs.

The facility is expected to be capable of producing traditional vehicles with internal combustion engines, all-electric vehicles and hybrid models, which have been growing in popularity in the U.S.

Honda declined to disclose what vehicles the company plans to produce at the new facility.

The plant is expected to increase Honda's light-duty vehicle capacity to 1.3 million units annually in the U.S. It currently has 12 manufacturing plants in United States, eight of which produce engines or light-duty vehicles for the auto industry.

"This is not simply an investment in another plant. It is an investment in the future of Honda manufacturing in America," Bob Nelson, executive vice president of American Honda Motor, said during the announcement at the company's U.S. heritage center in Ohio.

The auto industry has been trying to localize more production amid President Donald Trump's tariffs and inflated logistics costs that have cut into profits.

Nelson told CNBC after the event that tariffs are affecting the company's business but the new plant is more about Honda's ambitions to grow in the U.S.

"Tariffs are an impact to us, even with the high localization rate that we have," he said. "But the real impact is that we have customers out there wanting Honda product and we can't give it to them. That's the real area."

Nelson said Honda did not work with the Trump administration ahead of Monday's announcement.

Honda's plans for a new plant come roughly two weeks after Volkswagen-owned luxury brand Audi announced it would invest more than $4 billion in a new plant at a rally with President Donald Trump, which Audi CEO Gernot Dollner faced criticism for attending.

Honda's new plant is the company's first new mass production U.S. vehicle assembly plant since it opened a facility in Indiana in 2008. It also opened a $70 million performance vehicle facility in 2016 and a multibillion-dollar joint venture battery plant earlier this year, both in Ohio.

The announcement comes after the automaker in May indefinitely suspended an $11 billion investment plan to produce all-electric vehicles and batteries in Canada. It also canceled three planned EVs for the U.S. market.

American Honda Motor Co. executive Jennifer Thomas recently emphasized the Japanese automaker's commitment to the U.S. and need for additional capacity.

"In order to be competitive in this market, you're going to need to build products here," Thomas, senior vice president of corporate affairs, said Oct. 27 in Detroit. "We are focused on trying to maximize the existing capacity that we have, but we are at capacity, and so we're exploring all options."

Thomas also warned the automotive industry faces a "catastrophic" scenario if the U.S. and Canada cannot reach a new trade deal by the end of this year, when Trump has threatened to double tariffs on Canadian vehicles and parts from 25% to 50% on Jan. 1 if the two countries don't reach a deal.

Honda said it has built more than 33 million vehicles and more than 39 million engines in the U.S. since it started auto production in the U.S. in 1982.

The Japanese automaker currently has more than 30,000 employees in the U.S.

#502. Exclusive: Fairphone Lands Its First US Carrier Partner in Boost Mobile

Americans overwhelmingly buy their smartphones at carrier stores on contract—nearly 70 percent, according to 2025 IDC data—so any growing phone manufacturer must partner up to succeed, rather than rely solely on online retailers like Amazon.

That's why Fairphone, the only mobile manufacturer in the world with a 10/10 repairability rating for its smartphones, has announced its first carrier partnership with Boost Mobile. Fairphone has sold its smartphones in Europe for more than a decade, but it only began selling its phones in the US this summer.

Boost is a mobile virtual network operator (MVNO) formerly owned by Sprint and Dish before Dish merged with EchoStar. Unlike carriers such as AT&T, T-Mobile, or Verizon, MVNOs lease network access and don't own the underlying infrastructure; Boost primarily relies on AT&T's network for coverage, though it has some access to T-Mobile. While Americans largely rely on the big three carriers, Counterpoint research director Mohit Agrawal tells WIRED that around 16 percent of US consumers rely on MVNOs, including carrier-owned MVNOs like Mint Mobile and Cricket.

The $650 Fairphone Gen. 6+ is the only cellular device Fairphone sells in the US, and it’s certified to work on T-Mobile’s or AT&T’s networks. With the Boost Mobile partnership, you still can’t directly buy it from Boost's online storefront or brick-and-mortar stores. Instead, you'll have to buy the device from Fairphone or Amazon, then activate it through Boost with any of the MVNO's plans. Fairphone's website will have Boost plan options at purchase. If you go that route, you'll receive an eSIM activation email when the phone is delivered.

Rutger Sneep, chief commercial officer at Fairphone, tells WIRED in a text message that this is phase one, a first step in a long-term collaboration, and to expect more news soon on “joint go-to-market plans.” When asked whether Boost will facilitate repairs, since many Americans bring their devices to carrier stores for fixes, Sneep says Americans won't need to go anywhere to get their phone fixed.

“You can simply and affordably order all spare parts to your doorstep and carry out the repair yourself,” Sneep says. “In the more complex cases, which are also more rare, you only need to send your Fairphone to a repair center. Fortunately, within five years after purchasing your Gen. 6+, you're also still covered by your warranty.” (You need to register the phone within 90 days to get the full extended warranty.)

The Gen. 6+ comes with a screwdriver, and owners can replace 12 modular elements, from the USB-C port and battery to the screen and cameras. Fairphone sells spare parts on its website, and the components are affordable. For example, a replacement battery is $40, and a new display costs $90. For an iPhone 17, a battery replacement costs $99, and a display repair costs $329.

The Fairphone isn’t just repairable—the company also touts its products as sustainable and promotes fair wages for workers in the supply chain. (It has various independent accreditations, like B Corp certification and a Platinum EcoVadis rating.) These efforts are partly why the Gen. 6+ costs as much as it does; going by specs alone, you can get a competing device for less cash.

The overall smartphone market is declining, according to Counterpoint data, largely because of rising costs stemming from the memory crunch. Shipments declined 19 percent year over year, though smartphone revenue grew 3 percent in the third quarter of 2026 due to higher prices. Yes, phone makers have raised smartphone prices, a broader trend that is affecting all electronics. Fairphone claims it’s in a bright spot, though, with phone sales up 74 percent in the first half of the year and 48 percent growth in the second quarter alone.

#500. Goldman CEO Solomon says AI financing remains strong despite market jitters

Goldman Sachs CEO David Solomon told CNBC on Monday that financing demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite recent market jitters as investors reassess the risks involving the massive build-out.

Solomon said that his firm has been "very active" in AI capital markets deals over recent weeks, a setup that he expects to continue.

"There's still very strong demand for investments in the AI infrastructure build and the technology around the growth of AI," Solomon told CNBC's Leslie Picker.

Changes in the political environment are among factors that will "create recalibrations and ups and downs, and we're seeing some of that," Solomon said. "But I still deeply believe in the long-term trajectory of the power of AI in our economy to drive productivity."

AI-related stocks, including chipmakers and neocloud providers, have tumbled in recent weeks as investors reassess whether the hundreds of billions of dollars being poured into AI infrastructure will generate good returns.

The selloff, which began before the U.S. midterm elections, intensified after Democrats scored sweeping victories on Tuesday, raising concerns that political opposition could slow the industry's expansion.

The shifting sentiment comes at a key moment for AI model lab Anthropic, which is preparing for a potential IPO that could become one of the biggest tests yet of public-market appetite for AI companies. Goldman is among the banks leading that IPO process.

"We'll see, obviously, if this IPO moves forward, how it all plays out," Solomon said, declining to comment in detail about the IPO. "I would say that the market environment is still pretty constructive."

Solomon also pushed back against political resistance to data center construction, arguing that the infrastructure is essential to the U.S. economy and that it can be built responsibly.

"We certainly don't want this infrastructure going to places outside the U.S., which is what will naturally happen because the demand for compute is there," Solomon said.

Target: Target hasn't released its full Black Friday lineup yet, but we do know its first Early Black Friday sale kicks off Nov. 5 to Nov. 7. #496. Jensen Huang Resigns as Nvidia CEO

Jensen Huang stepped down as the chief executive of Nvidia on Thursday, four days after the company announced its internal investigation into an affair he had with Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, an alleged asset of China’s Ministry of State Security.

“Jensen Huang has resigned as President, Chief Executive Officer, and a member of the Board, effective immediately,” the Nvidia board said in a statement. Nvidia’s chief financial officer, Colette Kress, will serve as interim CEO. The board said it “will consider internal and external candidates” for a permanent replacement.

The board’s investigation was announced on November 8, slightly less than two weeks after The New York Times reported that the chief executive had been the target of a Chinese intelligence operation facilitated through his relationship with Alice Huang, who has no known relation to Jensen. The pair have been spotted in public together at least twice, in June 2024 and May 2025. On November 11, The New York Times reported that Jensen Huang also allegedly had a relationship with a direct report at Nvidia.

The alleged relationship with a subordinate, along with Huang’s business dealings with Megaspeed, a Singapore-based AI company where Alice Huang previously held a senior position, likely played a greater role in the board’s decision to oust Huang than the Chinese espionage allegations, a source familiar with the matter tells WIRED.

US intelligence officials reportedly briefed President Donald Trump on Huang’s relationship with Alice last year, but the president did not act on it. On November 6, The New York Times and Financial Times reported that Alice was a spy for China’s Ministry of State Security and that she had been recruited to collect information on the Nvidia executive. (The Chinese government has rejected all claims that Alice was a spy for the country.)

Huang sent a companywide email on Sunday acknowledging his affair, apologizing to his family and Nvidia employees, and pledging cooperation with the internal investigation. The executive has been married to his wife, Lori Huang, since 1985, and both of the couple’s two children work at the company.

On Monday, WIRED exclusively reported that last year Huang began working with a crisis PR firm to manage his personal communications. In October 2025, those PR consultants started to be looped in on all press inquiries involving Huang, whether those inquiries were personal or professional in nature.

The timing lines up with a New York Times report that month that the US Commerce Department was investigating whether Alice Huang’s Megaspeed was helping Chinese companies get around American export controls with the help of a vast web of subsidiaries. The investigation was reportedly halted and transferred to the Justice Department after Nvidia general counsel Tim Teter inquired about it.

Huang has been a vocal critic of these export controls, which he argues unnecessarily constrain the market for American chips, create discrete Chinese and American tech ecosystems, and spur China to build competitive chips of its own. Huang has worked to develop a relationship with Trump in order to advocate for policies that are advantageous for Nvidia, including the idea of opening its markets to China.

Huang cofounded Nvidia in 1993 with friends Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem. The trio reportedly brainstormed their vision for the company in a Denny’s diner and aspired to bring realistic 3D graphics to personal computers. For the first few decades of its existence, Nvidia was primarily known in the gaming community for creating chips powerful enough to render high-quality in-game graphics.

In 2012, after a new kind of neural network known as AlexNet won a high-profile prize in computer vision, Nvidia pivoted from high-speed computer graphics to building chips that would support artificial intelligence. Huang has repeatedly said that the introduction of AlexNet, which was developed by AI engineers Alex Krizhevsky, Ilya Sutskever, and Geoffrey Hinton, was a “Big Bang”–like event for computing.

Because its new GPUs proved highly effective at powering AI models that require massive amounts of processing power, the value of Nvidia shares soared in 2023 and 2024, in lockstep with the broader AI boom that began sweeping through the world economy. In the summer of 2025, Nvidia became the first company in history to cross $4 trillion in value.

As of September 2026, the company controlled about 90 percent of the GPU market. Much of Nvidia’s epic rise over the few decades has been attributed not just to its cutting-edge chips and its software “moat,” CUDA, that keeps developers locked into those chips, but also to the fast-thinking, controlling, charismatic, and often bombastic leadership style of Huang himself. Now, that critical part of the company’s success is out.

#493. Boeing to cut 5% of commercial airplane workers as production increase takes longer than expected

Boeing said Monday that it plans to cut 5% of its commercial airplane workforce as its production ramp-up is moving at a slower pace than the company expected.

The cuts will be through a combination of attrition and layoffs, Stephanie Pope, head of the commercial aircraft unit, said in a memo to staff.

Pope reiterated that the company is still working to stabilize production rates of its bestselling 737 Max planes, which are made outside of Seattle.

CEO Kelly Ortberg told investors last month that the company won't significantly increase production of the Max aircraft until the second half of next year. He said the company's wing production has been slower than expected.

"Adjusting our production schedules and reducing aircraft output puts us in an overstaffed position," Pope said in her note Monday. "Unfortunately, that means we need to align our staffing levels to our revised business plan, resulting in a reduction of approximately 5% through a combination of attrition and involuntary layoffs, with notifications beginning in December."

Boeing is targeting a production rate of 52 planes a month in the second half of 2027. Ortberg said the company has been trying to stabilize output at a rate of about 47 of its 737 Max planes a month.

#489. Apple’s HomeView Tries Face Authentication Without FaceID

FaceID is built for the use case where a person is staring at their device head-on from less than an arm’s length away. It provides reliable security that’s difficult to defeat because it stores your biometric data on a special, physically isolated chip known as the Secure Enclave—first introduced in 2013 for the iPhone 5s. The architecture is so robust that Apple also relies on the Secure Enclave as the foundation of a device’s entire encryption scheme. But Apple concluded that FaceID wasn’t the best fit for the HomeView, because it needs to bounce quickly between users and relies on AI processing, which can’t all be squeezed into the Secure Enclave.

As a result, HomeView uses Apple’s recently announced Secure Exclave (note the “x”) to handle the video and audio data the device uses to evaluate who is in a room at a given time. Apple says it has actually been laying the groundwork for the Secure Exclave for a long time—early versions of the architecture were already in place for the iPhone 16 and 17 and MacBook Neo. Instead of being an entirely separately chip, the Secure Exclave is a dedicated, memory-protected space in recent Apple processors. It is in Apple’s latest S11 chip for Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4, as well as the A20 Pro chip for iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo.

Even though the Secure Exclave isn’t based on the tried and true Secure Enclave technology, Apple argues that it is robust and expands the company’s ability to secure a range of features, including improving the security of existing mechanisms. In a blog post on Thursday, the company even goes into some detail about how it used the mathematical technique known as formal verification to vet the “microkernel” designed for the Secure Exclave.

“This work both benefits our newest features and strengthens the security of features we already ship, such as ‘Hey Siri’ detection,” the company writes in a blog post. “Our goal is to keep ambient sensor data secure even against highly sophisticated attacks.”

HomeView also uses the Secure Exclave to govern the indicator light that tells users when their camera or microphone is active. The MacBook Neo uses the Exclave in this same way, though only to indicate when the camera is active, not the microphone. The goal is to isolate this process and the chain of command governing it to make it even more difficult for attackers to try to disable an indicator light when it should be on or stop it from coming on in the first place.

The HomeView’s “person recognition” technology is simply not as comprehensive as FaceID, though, and it is possible that the technology is more susceptible to reconstructed 3D depth map attacks—and potentially voiceprint attacks—than FaceID.

Of course, you don’t have to have a HomeView in your house. Or if you do, you can use it on guest mode or a less involved “personalized” mode that only incorporates things like your playlists for other users to access as well and doesn’t access calendar or Messages data. But as Apple forges deeper into AI integration, HomeView’s departure from FaceID provides insight into the scale of the changes Apple is planning.

Updated 11/12/2026 at 11:35 am ET to clarify when the MacBook Neo indicator light is verified by the Secure Exclave.

#487. These are the 20 best books of 2026, according to Amazon editors

Amazon just dropped its best books of 2026 list. Earlier this year, the editors over at Amazon rounded up the best books of the year so far, but now that the year is nearly wrapped, the definitive list is here.

"We saw people reach for sweeping family sagas, genre-bending fiction, gripping narrative nonfiction, and horror," says Amazon Editor Al Woodworth. The end-of-year list is packed with books across genres, and this year, the editors at Amazon even added two new best-of lists on horror and buzzworthy books.

Picking the top 20 books of the year is a big task. Woodworth says that Amazon Editors are reading thousands of books every year, then championing the titles they can't stop thinking about. "At the end of the year, we look back at everything we've read and debate which books belong on the final list. Every reader is different, so we recommend books from the widest possible selection," says Woodworth.

There are common themes across the top books of the year, including non-fiction that reads like fiction, stories of sacrificing for those they love, characters facing hard choices, and books the internet can't stop talking about.

But the best book of the year? Well, read on to find out which novel Woodworth and fellow Amazon Editors declared the best of the year.

Earlier this year, Amazon Editor Annabel Gutterman spoke on Night Objects, saying: “This mesmerizing suspense novel about a teenager’s desperate search for belonging at her elite boarding school — where secrets swirl, and danger looms large — lived in my head for days. Eli Raphael introduces herself as a writer you don’t want to miss.”

Fans of Alison Bechdel's Fun Home will love the 2026 release The Nightingales by Emil Wilson. This graphic novel is a touching picture of unlikely friendship between a 17-year-old girl and the dying stranger who moves into her family home. It's visually and emotionally striking, a graphic novel that's full of hope.

The Disappearers came out with a bang as the New York Times declared it "the novel of the decade, if not the century." It's a propulsive crime story that manages to be both harrowing and hilarious as it captures the gay experience in 1980s Jamaica.

From Gutterman: “When a tradwife influencer wakes up to discover it’s somehow the 19th century, her journey back to reality makes for a brilliant, biting novel about motherhood, fame, and faith. A reading experience like no other, this is the book I want to talk about with literally everyone I've ever met.”

From the author of Shuggie Bain, Douglas Stuart, comes a new novel, John of John. When John-Calum 'Cal' Macleod returns to his home on the Isle of Harris, it is not the place that has changed, but him. At odds with his father's expectations, Cal discovered the secrets within his home and what's truly holding the community together.

Earlier this year, Sarah Gelman, Amazon Editorial Director, said: "Strangers is a divorce memoir, but it’s also a forensic examination of a love and a marriage gone wrong, seemingly without any warning. Burden puts words to many of our worst fears — that one day the person we love and trust the most will become an utter stranger to us.”

As a romance reader, there's one book I haven't stopped hearing about. Bad Words by Rioghnach Robinson is a will-they-won't-they romance of a novelist and the critic who panned his debut book. But don't take it from me. Emily Henry calls this novel a "once-in-a-lifetime read."

As if releasing the hit film, Teenage Sex and Death at Camp Miasma wasn't enough, director Jane Schoenbrun also released the novel Public Access Afterworld. The debut is being heralded as one of the top books of the year as it delves into the surreal broadcasts of Public Access Afterworld, a pirate TV network with the potential to change lives.

Luna, Phoenix, Queen is an intricate novel, unraveling the relationship between two college professors and their resilient dog, Luna. Julie Orringer's latest novel is a web of artistic and marital betrayal, and of all those tangled in that story.

The popularity of epic family sagas continues with Mary Beth Keane's Whale Harbor, a novel told across the globe. Set in Ireland, England, Montana, and New York, the story is based on Keane's actual family history following the haunting mystery that hangs over a family when one of its members disappears.

From Woodworth: “Two-time Pulitzer Prize winner Colson Whitehead delivers another searing page-turner of heists, gangsters, criminals, and families in this mesmerizing portrait of 1980s New York City.”

From Lindsay Powers, Amazon Editor: “This heart-pounding, reported memoir sucked me in from the first page; it’s a gripping account of resilience and perseverance that goes far beyond the headlines to reveal what really happened to Evan Gershkovich when he was snatched off the street and thrown into a Russian prison.”

From Gelman: “The many fans of Big Little Lies will love this follow-up, but you don’t need to have read that book or seen the show to enjoy Big Little Truths. I’ve been a fan of Liane Moriarty since her early books, and this is simply one of her very best. The dialogue is so clever, and the storytelling so engrossing, you won’t be able to put this book down.”

From Woodworth: “Illuminating and unforgettable, Patrick Radden Keefe’s latest delivers another knockout work of narrative nonfiction, this time telling the shocking story of how a young man got caught up in the dangerous and powerful world of Russian oligarchs and landed at the bottom of the Thames.”

Woodworth says, “Never before have a 19th-century mapmaker, a scruffy, loyal dog, and a mysterious copse of trees been more enchanting. O’Farrell delivers a soaring and bewitching tale of family, and how the remote Irish peninsula where they live defines and shapes them, generation after generation.”

From Abby Abell, Amazon Editor: "Walking the line between hysterical and horrifying, this electric psychological thriller ruthlessly takes on cultural appropriation through a delicious cat-and-mouse game like no other. If you’re in a reading slump, Fruit Fly is the cure.”

From Erin Kodicek, Amazon Editor: “This was our pick for the best book of the year so far, and our love for this novel about two motherless friends coming of age in the Jim Crow South endures. A nuanced portrait of friendship, family, and race — Jones's sentences sing.”

#485. PepsiCo to cut about 1,000 jobs as North American business struggles

PepsiCo is cutting about 1,000 jobs across North America as the company tries to turn around lagging sales in its home market.

"We have a clear ambition for PepsiCo North America: to build a stronger, more future-fit business that delivers industry-leading growth," Ram Krishnan, CEO of PepsiCo North America, wrote in a Monday letter to employees obtained by CNBC. "To do that, we need to be faster, more connected, and more capable, while continuing to invest in growth — behind our people, capabilities and brands."

Over the next week, Pepsi plans to cut jobs as it says it aims to streamline its operations and better connect teams across the company's North American business. PepsiCo employs about 306,000 people worldwide, including roughly 125,000 in the United States, as of Dec. 27, according to a company filing.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the coming layoffs.

In October, PepsiCo cut its full-year earnings outlook, citing the struggles in its home market. During the third quarter, its North American beverage unit saw volume shrink 2%, while its North American food division reported flat volume.

While the company has tried to boost sales by cutting prices and investing in marketing, the turnaround is taking longer than expected, Chief Financial Officer Steve Schmitt said on the company's earnings conference call.

Shares of Pepsi rose slightly in afternoon trading. The stock is down nearly 1% this year, lagging behind the S&P 500's gains of nearly 11% over the same period.

#483. Here’s How Google Says It’s Making Its Smart Glasses More Private

Smart glasses have a public perception problem. People wearing Meta's popular Ray-Ban glasses have been labeled “Meta Creeps.” But it didn't start with Meta—when Google Glass launched in 2013, “Glasshole” was the word used to describe people wearing them.

It boils down to the fact that these smart glasses have cameras on them that let people record whatever’s in front of them with the push of a button. The cameras aren't easy to see, and while an indicator light activates when the glasses are recording, they're far less conspicuous than someone holding a smartphone up to record video or photos. It also doesn't help that people have tried to tamper with the LEDs and block them, and have recorded women without their consent.

But Google is on a mission to change the perception of smart glasses as socially intrusive tech. The first Android XR Intelligent Eyewear smart glasses—made in collaboration with Samsung, Warby Parker, and Gentle Monster—are now available for preorder, with sales kicking off on December 10. You can read about how they work and what they can do in this separate story.

Here's a collection of the things Google says it's doing to make its smart glasses more private:

Payne hopes these policies and actions will encourage other companies to follow suit. “We hope that never again do glasses launch from some other company that doesn't have good algorithms built in,” he says. “That should not have happened; hopefully it doesn't happen again.”

Reem Suleiman, senior campaign director at the nonprofit digital rights advocacy group Fight for the Future, says camera-equipped glasses shouldn't be normalized in public because they are a precursor to mass surveillance and have the capacity to identify people. (This summer, Meta silently added a face-recognition code to its smart glasses and then subsequently deleted it after a WIRED report; in 2024, two Harvard students built a face-recognition tool for Meta's glasses.)

“People find ways around it,” Suleiman says. “There's no actual aspect of consent.”

Intriguingly, Payne highlighted that tech companies like Google need to make clear, declarative statements about what the glasses are doing.

“Ambiguity is not your friend here,” Payne says. “I think that a lot of that concern came from some trust being broken. So there is a rebuilding thing happening right now … You can hopefully have your cake and eat it too, which is cool glasses that everybody's excited about—that you're comfortable with.”

#482. Jensen Huang resigns: A timeline of the Nvidia affair

The AI boom has put chatbots at work, AI-generated videos in our feeds, and data centers on the agenda at town halls. Nvidia, a company that got its start making computer games look better, now supplies the chips that power much of the AI industry. That made Nvidia one of the world's most valuable companies and its chips some of the most sought-after technology in the world.

And on Thursday, Nov. 12, the company announced that it had lost the only CEO it had ever had.

The story goes back more than a year and reads a little like an HBO drama, with alleged spies, extramarital affairs, and corporate intrigue. Let's break down the timeline of the Jensen Huang resignation based on what came to light and when.

Last year, the New York Times reported that U.S. officials were looking into whether Nvidia chips meant for data centers in Southeast Asia had ended up in China.

The chips in question had been bought by Megaspeed, a Singapore company that rented out computing power. U.S. export rules limit China's access to some of Nvidia’s most advanced AI hardware, to protect Nvidia's trade secrets and the United States' competitive edge over China in the AI arms race. Singapore police were also looking into whether Megaspeed had broken local laws, but at the time, Nvidia said it had no reason to believe the chips had been sent elsewhere.

The Times' report referred to Megaspeed's "mystery C.E.O." and noted U.S. officials were looking into the company's ties to China.

Things were quiet for a while; until two weeks ago, when the Times reported that U.S. intelligence officials suspected Alice Huang, a former Megaspeed executive, of gathering information for Beijing. Officials had learned that Alice Huang had developed a close personal relationship with Jensen Huang. (Yes, the two share a surname. No, they are not related.)

Nvidia initially characterized their interactions as routine meetings with a customer: "Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected," an Nvidia spokesperson told TechCrunch in an email on Oct. 29, adding that "meetings with customers are part of a C.E.O.’s job."

The Times also reported that intelligence officials had briefed President Donald Trump about the relationship roughly a year earlier. By then, Jensen Huang had already spent months pressing the Trump administration to let Nvidia sell more advanced AI chips to China.

Flash forward to Nov. 5, when the chairs of three House committees publicly asked Jensen Huang for a closed-door briefing. They wanted to know whether his ties to Alice Huang could have given Beijing access to sensitive U.S. technology or information, and what Nvidia was doing to guard against foreign espionage.

The next day, the New York Times reported that Secretary of State Marco Rubio had overseen the drafting of a classified memo this spring. It described U.S. intelligence suggesting that China's Ministry of State Security had recruited Alice Huang to target the Nvidia CEO. As the chairs of the House Foreign Affairs wrote, "Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information." Meanwhile, the Financial Times reported that U.S. intelligence officials believed the relationship was romantic. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Want more tech and digital culture news delivered to your inbox daily or sent straight to your device? Sign up for Mashable's Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

On Sunday, Nov. 8, Nvidia made the rumors official. The company published an email Huang sent to employees. "I was unfaithful to my wife," he wrote. "There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry."

He did not name Alice Huang, though the New York Times reported that he'd privately told Nvidia’s board he had been intimate with her. Huang said he would cooperate with the investigation. A few hours later, Nvidia's board announced the investigation in a separate statement and said its independent directors would oversee it with outside counsel. The board said it had no reason at that point to believe national security or company secrets had been compromised and expressed confidence in Huang and his leadership team.

On Monday, Trump told reporters he'd asked Jensen Huang whether he knew about the spying allegations involving Alice Huang. "He said 'I had no idea that she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump said. China's foreign ministry spokesperson, Guo Jiakun, dismissed the accusation. "The 'Chinese spy' allegation is purely unsubstantiated and directed at smearing China," he exclaimed.

That same day, Nvidia shareholder Mark Kistenmacher filed a proposed class action alleging that the company and its CEO had misled investors about Huang's ties to the former Megaspeed executive.

The next day, Nvidia's independent directors said they'd hired Joon H. Kim, a former acting U.S. attorney in Manhattan, and his firm, Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, to lead their investigation.

The story continued to unravel Wednesday, as the New York Times reported that Huang also had an affair with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare and one of his direct reports (of which he has more than 60). The Wall Street Journal later reported that Nvidia was preparing to remove him, and Reuters reported that the board met that evening to discuss his future.

On Thursday morning, Nvidia officially announced Huang's departure after 33 years as CEO and named longtime CFO Colette Kress interim CEO. An SEC filing released that morning said the board had accepted Huang's resignation as CEO, president, and director the previous evening, effective immediately. Kress will keep her finance role while the board considers internal and external candidates for the permanent job. Nvidia also named longtime director Harvey C. Jones its first board chair.

Also on Thursday, Singapore's home affairs ministry said Alice Huang had left the country in February and had been barred from re-entering following a local investigation involving possible offenses tied to server purchases and exports. The ministry said investigators found insufficient evidence to establish that she had committed an offense, and that its inquiry did not concern espionage. Megaspeed, for its part, said it had cooperated with U.S. inspections and hired a law firm to independently review its export compliance.

Nvidia is scheduled to release its quarterly results around 4:20 p.m. ET on Tuesday, Nov. 17, followed by an earnings call at 5 p.m. ET. It will be the company’s first earnings call since Jensen Huang resigned and Kress took over as interim CEO.

#477. Google and Samsung’s Android XR Smart Glasses Are Finally Here

Google and Samsung's next face computers have arrived. Nearly two years after the companies first announced the Android XR platform that powers the hardware, the smart glasses are finally on sale.

The “Intelligent Eyewear” is a collaboration between the two tech companies, with Samsung providing its hardware expertise and Google handling much of the software platform. Smart glasses are just one part of Android XR—there are also mixed-reality headsets and glasses for more immersive experiences, like last year's Samsung Galaxy XR and the upcoming Xreal Aura.

Eyewear brands Warby Parker and Gentle Monster are heavily involved in the new smart glasses, responsible for the design and fit, similar to how Meta tapped Ray-Ban for its competing—and market-leading—wearables. Google and Samsung are leveraging Warby Parker and Gentle Monster's brick-and-mortar presence and retail expertise, which lets people demo the glasses before buying them. (Kering Eyewear, which owns brands like Lindberg and Maui Jim, has also signed up to make glasses for Android XR in the future.)

The first models are available for preorder now, with official sales starting on December 10. Warby Parker's frames start at $395 and come in three styles—Alden, Reid, and Wren—as optical or sunglasses, with various prescription, lens, and width options. Gentle Monster's glasses start at $449 and come in six designs, various colors, and tints, with prescription options for most frames.

The hardware is identical across both brands. The glasses weigh around 41 grams (10 grams less than Meta's new Gen 3 glasses), have a nine-hour battery life, and include a charging case that can recharge the glasses up to seven times. They have a 12-megapixel camera with an LED indicator that shows everyone in eyeshot that you’re recording. There’s a button to capture photos and 90-second videos at up to 3K resolution with a press. A touch-sensitive temple piece lets you tap to activate the onboard Gemini assistant, or slide two fingers back and forth to control the volume of the audio coming out of the glasses' speaker.

They are the first devices to support the “Hey Gemini” hotword, though you can use the well-known “Hey Google” hotword if you'd like. Still, the new spoken wake phrase distinguishes the glasses as Gemini's “optimal form,” according to Juston Payne, Google's senior director of product management for XR.

“We felt that these are a Gemini-forward device,” Payne tells WIRED. “It's really you and Gemini going through your day together, so it was feeling a little dissonant for us. You're not talking to Google, you're talking to Gemini.”

Oddly, these glasses are only compatible with certain devices—Google Pixel phones, Samsung Galaxy phones, and iPhones—and only select models. You'll need a Pixel 7 or newer, Galaxy S or Z Fold running Samsung's One UI 9.0 or higher, and the iPhone 13 Pro or newer. A Google spokesperson tells WIRED the company will share more about how it plans to expand support to other phones in 2027 after launch.

The arms of the glasses are similarly thick.

Since you'll be buying the glasses from an eyewear brand, they will be the customer-facing party to handle any problems you may have with the device or prescriptions. On the backend, Payne says there are elaborate business integrations and handoffs, so the customer service workload is split between all the companies.

Google has already previewed Intelligent Eyewear glasses with displays built into the lenses—useful for showing navigation instructions or real-time captions for folks with hearing issues. However, they are not ready yet; Payne says they're currently being tested by “non-Googlers” and are still on the road map, though he didn't share a specific release window. As for plans to launch audio-only smart glasses, like Meta's recent Ray-Ban Audio, Warby Parker co-CEO Dave Gilboa tells WIRED, “There's a pretty robust pipeline of additional products.”

Smart glasses have been around for a few years, but they come in many forms. Unlike display smart glasses designed to plug into a laptop to mirror or extend your screen, or more immersive glasses like Meta's VR Glasses, which layer virtual experiences over the real world, Intelligent Eyewear is more akin to what Google pitched years ago with the original Google Glass.

You can hear notifications pipe through from your connected smartphone and respond to messages by speaking to Gemini. You can listen to music, take phone calls, and get audio navigation through Google Maps. Yes, you can snap photos and videos too, though Payne quickly notes that these glasses are designed more to have Gemini assist you throughout the day than to capture content.

That's likely because Meta has faced public scrutiny over how people use its smart glasses, so much so that it has spawned a new term for people wearing them: the Meta Creep (harkening back to when anyone wearing Google Glasses was called a “Glasshole”).

“We learned through Glass that you can make people uncomfortable if you're not clearly communicating,” Payne says.

Gentle Monster's Intelligent Eyewear comes in six different styles.

Most of the features that Google is positioning around Intelligent Eyewear rely on Gemini. See a poster for a comedy show? Just ask Gemini for more information, or add it to your calendar. You can ask Gemini to add items to your shopping list or whip up a travel itinerary with hotel recommendations and save them to a note. Some of these are saved in the Gemini app, but it can leverage other apps on your smartphone. For example, Gemini can play music on Apple Music if you're on an iPhone. And if you use Samsung Notes on a Samsung phone, it'll know that's where you likely want your notes saved.

When you activate Gemini, it's technically a “Gemini Live” experience, meaning the assistant activates and you can have a free-flowing conversation. A swipe back on the glasses' arm will end the interaction. When you want it to “look” at something in front of you, be specific in your query; otherwise, it won't use the camera. You can ask it to look at a page in a book and summarize the content, or if you have a bunch of cocktail ingredients in front of you but don't know what to make, you can ask Gemini for suggestions and step-by-step instructions to craft a drink—at your pace.

Some Gemini controls are on-device and don't need to travel to the cloud, like asking the assistant to play the next track. Speaking of, early next year, Google says users can use gestures on devices like the Fitbit Air or Google Pixel Watch to control music playback or activate Gemini on the glasses right from their wrist.

Another big capability is translation. Gemini will automatically detect languages and translate them to your preferred tongue. You do need to stare at the individual to hear their words translated, and there's a limited range; the quality depends heavily on the environment and noise level, but it worked remarkably well in my controlled demos, with only a few errors. The translation tries to mimic the speaker's tone and voice so it feels like their own words.

There's an accessibility feature called Guided Vision, which provides audio descriptions for people with impaired vision, like when reading fine print or locating everyday items. Google is also collaborating with Aira so you can stream the glasses' feed straight to a human interpreter for real-time visual support.

To help demystify these features—and convince consumers that smart glasses aren't just a fad for tech bros—Warby Parker and Gentle Monster are both launching pop-up experiences in New York City this week. Gentle Monster's pop-up will run for about a month, but Warby Parker's “Literatureland” will be open through 2027, letting people walk through the experience with a pair of nonprescription glasses to demo Gemini's capabilities.

“These are the original wearables that have been around for hundreds of years,” Warby Parker's Gilboa says. “A lot of people start by getting an eye exam in one of our stores, and then come in, do a demo, buy these—we just want to make it as easy and seamless as possible.”

#475. Apple says it will shut down its Apple Support Community forums after 28 years

If you've ever had a problem with your MacBook, iPhone, or Apple account, a quick Google search for a fix likely sent you to a relevant thread on the Apple Support Community at discussions.apple.com, where Apple customers tried to help their fellow Apple fans out.

On Tuesday, Apple announced that it would be closing down the Apple Support Community, the official forum for Apple customers looking for help from other Apple users with their Apple products.

Apple disabled posting in the Apple Support Community on Tuesday. The platform is still viewable in a read-only state. However, the forums will be shuttered for good on Nov. 20.

A prompt now appears at the top of the Apple Support Community website and all of its posts with Apple's announcement of the forum's closure: The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

"The Apple Support Community will permanently close on November 20, 2026. Starting today, the site will no longer accept new posts and is available in a read-only state.

You can continue to find trusted guidance and support on the official Apple Support site.

We want to thank the community members who have contributed their time and expertise to help others with their Apple products."

Anyone who has ever had an Apple product likely found themselves on the Apple Support Community at some point. Even if they didn't sign up to ask a question themselves, the answer to their question likely existed in the Apple Support Community archives, helping numerous customers avoid reaching out directly to Apple's official support.

The forum launched in 1998 as the Apple Tech Exchange. According to MacRumors, the forums went through numerous name changes over the years, going by Apple Discussion in 2000, before the company finally settled on the Apple Support Community in 2022.

The last post on the Apple Support Community appears to have been published by a user looking for help with their Apple account at 5:41 p.m. on Nov. 10. One last reply was posted in the thread minutes later at 5:49 p.m. Apple appears to have moved the forums to a read-only state shortly after that.

Apple reportedly told MacRumors that "it decided to sunset the Apple Support Community after a careful evaluation of strategy and customer needs."

What if you need help with your Apple products going forward? Apple suggests that you contact its official support directly.

#472. Children’s Place files for bankruptcy protection, seeks buyer

Children's clothing retailer The Children's Place has filed for chapter 11 bankruptcy protection, it said Sunday.

The company is looking to sell substantially all of its assets and is seeking offers from potential buyers by Dec. 9, with a goal to close a deal by the end of December.

The retailer, whose brands include The Children's Place and Gymboree, says it operates two digital storefronts and 514 North American stores, and has distribution in 13 countries through franchise and wholesale partners. The company's performance has taken a hit in recent years from tariff hikes and slower consumer spending, among other factors.

Its stores and e-commerce sites remain open for now, and the company is planning to continue its ongoing inventory sales with court approval.

"For nearly 60 years, The Children's Place has supported growing families with apparel that customers have come to know and love. We recognize the significance of this development for our employees, customers, vendors, and other partners, and we are committed to conducting an efficient and transparent sale process," said Rudy Morando, co-chief restructuring officer of The Children's Place. "In the meantime, we remain focused on serving the families who have trusted The Children's Place for generations."

This move comes days after interim Children's Place CEO Muhammad Asif Seemab and Chief Strategy Officer Muhammad Umair stepped down from their roles. Seemab and Umair will both remain on the company's board. CFO John Szczepanski took on the role of acting president and principal executive officer.

Seemab is also managing director of Saudi-based Mithaq Capital, which took a majority stake in The Children's Place in 2024 when the company was facing financial trouble.

Even with support from the investors, the company has not been able to significantly improve its financial outlook. In its bankruptcy filing, the company reported about $742 million in assets, compared to about $881 million in debts.

In the second fiscal quarter of 2026, its most recently reported quarter, the company's net sales dropped nearly 19% from the same period the year prior. At the time, Seemab said in a news release that during the quarter the company was "stabilizing our customer file" and "driving traffic to our stores and websites," and saw "lower conversion, combined with a very clearance-heavy mix."

He also pointed to tough comparisons to the prior-year period, due in part to back-to-school shopping demand that "was uniquely elevated ... ahead of anticipated tariff price increases."

The Children's Place stock has plummeted in recent years and was trading around 18 cents per share on Monday.

#471. Meet Colette Kress, Nvidia’s New Interim CEO

Nvidia’s CEO and cofounder Jensen Huang is officially out, and longtime chief financial officer Colette Kress is in. The chipmaker’s board of directors announced Thursday that Kress, who joined Nvidia in 2013, has been named interim CEO following Huang’s resignation amid a scandal over his extramarital affair with a Chinese businesswoman believed by US officials to be an asset of China’s Ministry of State Security.

Harvey Jones, the newly appointed chair of Nvidia’s board, said in a statement that Kress “has helped lead Nvidia through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position.” He added that Kress will be supported by an “experienced leadership team” and that the board has “full confidence” in her.

Kress was among the most obvious candidates to fill the void after Huang’s departure, according to multiple tech executives and industry analysts who follow Nvidia closely, given her long tenure at the company, credibility on Wall Street and across the tech industry, and, perhaps most importantly, her deep familiarity with Nvidia’s business and finances.

“She’s extremely strong-willed, professional, and whip-smart. She doesn’t tolerate nonsense. She can be friendly and gracious too,” says a former Nvidia executive who spoke on the condition of anonymity. “She obviously has a keen sense for how Jensen thinks.”

“Colette is the logical choice,” says Patrick Moorhead, founder and chief analyst at Moor Insights & Strategies, who has tracked the chip industry for decades. “She has credibility with investors because she answers hard questions directly.”

Moorhead says Kress has operated as much more than a “numbers” CFO. “In my conversations with her, she goes deep on the product road map, from rack-scale systems to networking and software,” he says.

Nvidia’s board said on Sunday it was launching an independent investigation into Huang’s activities following a report by The New York Times that he had an affair with a woman named Alice Huang, who US officials suspected of being a Chinese intelligence asset. WIRED reported on Monday that Huang quietly hired a crisis communications firm sometime last year to handle matters related to his personal life. By October of 2025, the firm was being looped in on all press inquiries involving Huang at Nvidia, around the same time The New York Times reported that US authorities were investigating whether a company linked to Alice Huang had helped Chinese firms evade US chip export controls through its subsidiaries.

While Huang has long been the public face of Nvidia, Kress has been responsible for managing the chipmaker’s relationship with Wall Street. On earnings calls, she often details Nvidia’s financial performance, customer demand, supply constraints, and other key metrics.

“One of the things we don’t focus on is the stock price. We focus on our investors and how we would like to innovate,” Kress told the Israeli business newspaper Globes in 2023.

Before joining Nvidia, Kress spent more than two decades in finance roles at Microsoft, Cisco, and Texas Instruments. Huang personally recruited her in 2013, when Nvidia was still best known for its gaming graphics cards, according to Globes. Huang laid out his vision for the company’s future on a whiteboard, explaining how Nvidia could become a major supplier of computing infrastructure for artificial intelligence.

Other potential successors to Huang included Ian Buck, an Nvidia veteran of more than 20 years who created the company’s software platform CUDA and now oversees its high-performance computing business, and Jeff Fisher, who joined Nvidia in 1994 as the head of software sales and later became senior vice president of its GeForce unit, where he led Nvidia’s strategy for bringing GPUs to market.

However, Nvidia’s unusual corporate structure has long meant that dozens of executives reported directly to Huang. The company lacks a traditional chief operating officer. Even executive vice presidents aren’t necessarily given direct control over budgets, sources have told WIRED. As a result, few people within the organization have the same level of understanding of the company’s financials as Kress.

The former Nvidia executive who spoke to WIRED on the condition of anonymity said that while Kress is incredibly sharp and “can step into the [CEO] role immediately without needing any guidance,” she doesn’t have the same engineering and technical expertise that Huang brought to the role. She has also shied away from being “at the center of public attention,” the person said.

Given the intense scrutiny surrounding Huang lately, that leadership style might be a welcome temporary change for Nvidia.

#467. Lucid misses Wall Street’s earnings expectations, expands loan from Saudi Arabia fund

Lucid Group missed Wall Street's third-quarter expectations as the embattled electric vehicle maker continues an "operational reset" aimed at cutting costs and narrowing losses.

The company on Monday also announced a $1.75 billion increase in a delayed draw term loan from Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the company's largest shareholder.

Lucid stock fell roughly 5% in aftermarket trading Monday.

Here's how Lucid performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Despite the top- and bottom-line misses, the company's adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of a loss of $726 million beat Wall Street's expectations of a loss of $611 million for the third quarter. Its net loss for the quarter was $830.9 million, down from a loss of $978.4 million a year earlier.

Lucid's 2025 third quarter included revenue of $336.6 million, a loss of $3.31 per share and an adjusted EBITDA loss of $717.7 million.

The quarterly results are the first since Lucid CEO Silvio Napoli, who started leading the company in June, announced a restructuring plan that included implementing several cost-cutting efforts and delaying the company's upcoming midsize vehicle from the end of this year until the second half of next.

"Our transformation is beginning to show progress. We are acting with urgency, strengthening the fundamentals and driving greater discipline, while continuing to invest in the priorities to deliver profitable long-term growth," Napoli said in a release.

The company's turnaround plan includes $1.4 billion in cash flow improvements this year as well as focusing attention on a robotaxi program with Uber and Nuro, a factory that's under construction in Saudi Arabia and the midsize vehicle.

Lucid said the capital from PIF is expected to give the company "additional financial flexibility as Lucid delivers on its turnaround program." It is expected to assist in funding "operations and key investments" into the fourth quarter of 2027, including the start of production for the midsize vehicle.

The additional funds boost Lucid's total liquidity to $3.75 billion, up from $2 billion to end the third quarter.

"We are grateful for the continued financial support from our anchor shareholder, which further energizes us to remain focused on execution and on our commitments as we build a stronger, sustainable Lucid," Napoli said.

Lucid said Monday that the company expects to communicate its midterm plan and targets early next year. The EV maker in May suspended its guidance and many targets, citing new leadership and time needed to develop a turnaround plan.

It features new footage that bridges the narrative between the two films, which Mashable's Belen Edwards graciously broke down for anyone who doesn't have the time for a rewatch.
