#464. Best Open Earbuds (2026): Shokz, Bose, Soundcore, and More

Open earbuds are the big new thing in headphone land. Unlike in-ear variants, open earbuds are like tiny speakers that rest on the ear and let outside sound in naturally, so you can fully hear the world around you. Some use a hook style that wraps around the back of your ear, while others are clip-ons that look more like statement-piece earrings. Style aside, they are excellent for anyone who finds traditional earbuds uncomfortable or wants full environmental awareness for activities like running, biking, or even just doing chores around the house.

I’ve tested dozens of models since 2022, and while I still recommend noise-canceling earbuds, I also love open earbuds for specific use cases.

For more headphone recommendations, check out our guides to the Best Wireless Earbuds, Best Workout Headphones, and Best Wireless Headphones.

Updated November 2026: I am recommending the Anker AeroClip 2 as the best overall, followed closely by the Shokz OpenDots. I’ve also added the Apple AirPods 5 and CMF Clip Pro as an honorable mention.

These are truly take-everywhere, do-anything headphones I would recommend to anyone looking for great open earbuds. They are exceedingly comfortable and easy to use all day, so much so that I sometimes forgot I was wearing them.

They’re clip-ons that wrap around the earlobe, with the speaker end resting at the ear canal opening and the other end behind the ear. Importantly, the top of your ear is free for glasses, sunglasses, or hats. Like nearly all open earbuds, they let in sound from your surroundings, so don’t expect them to be a great companion for long-haul flights or daily gym sessions.

There are other open earbuds that are nearly as good as these, and some surpass them in areas like sound quality. But it’s a combination of small details that puts the AeroClip 2 at the top. The physical button controls on each earbud are much easier to use than touch-sensitive alternatives, especially when running or exercising outdoors. The charging case is surprisingly small, smaller than most other options on this list. And, importantly, the call quality is great. These buds use the same AI-powered chip as the Soundcore Liberty 5 Pro, and while they don’t sound quite as good, they are noticeably better than other open earbuds, especially in noisy environments.

The best part? They cost less than many similar competitors I tested.

My former top pick, and still a great option, especially if you value battery life. These earbuds do most of what the Soundcore AeroClip 2 does, just a little larger and a little pricier. The buds are exceedingly comfortable and stable for all-day wear, even during outdoor workouts.

What sets them apart from the AeroClip 2 is battery life. They are best in class—10 hours on a single charge, and 40 hours total with the charging case. They also have an IP57 dust and water-resistance rating, the same as premium, flagship earbuds like Apple AirPods Pro 3 and Samsung Galaxy Buds4 Pro. I’ve worn them in the rain, on the beach, and fully submerged them in water multiple times without issue.

They are a bit pricey, though. Shokz recently launched the lower-priced Shokz OpenDots Air and Shokz OpenFit Air 2—I may update this recommendation depending on how my testing goes.

The Bose Ultra Open Earbuds were a big deal when they launched in 2024—a brand-new design from Bose and one of the biggest swings from a major brand in clip-on open earbuds. Two years later, the brand launched the second-generation version, and they are a solid, if expensive, option. That’s mainly because of sound quality—these earbuds sound clearer and more defined than most competitors. Another differentiator is physical button controls on each bud, which I find easier to use than touch alternatives, especially when running or biking.

Like the Soundcore and Shokz earbuds, they are very comfortable, so much so that I sometimes forgot I am wearing them. This gen-two version has a more distinct, two-tone material design that is certainly eye-catching, especially in the white colorway. They also now support wireless charging, a big omission in the first generation. But they are much pricier by comparison, and only worth it if you want the best sound quality in this clip-on form factor.

It can be a little nerve-wracking to spend big bucks on a concept (open earbuds) you aren’t totally convinced by. That’s one reason I love this $50 option—the PearClip Pros aren’t super pricey and are a great way to find out if you even like open earbuds in the first place, especially the kind that clip onto the outside of your ear.

They are far less refined than Shokz or Soundcore. Things like the app and touch controls can be a little finicky. But that’s common at this super-low price point, and you can always upgrade to a more premium earbud if you fall in love with the clip-on form factor.

The Shokz OpenRun Pro 2 are more like headphones than earbuds—with a wraparound headband, long battery life, great sound quality (for open headphones), and no charging case. They use a combination of bone conduction (think vibrations) and air conduction (think tiny speakers pointed toward your ears) to deliver clearer sound than most smaller open earbuds, especially in noisy environments. That combination of technologies is why these are some of my favorite earbuds for outdoor fitness activities like running and biking.

I do wish these headphones were a little cheaper. I’m currently testing the recently released, more affordable Shokz OpenRun Air 2, and I’m hopeful they can be a budget-friendly alternative.

Apple AirPods 5 With Wireless Charging Case for $149: Apple AirPods 5 are semi-open earbuds—they still go in your ears, but don’t have rubber eartips that block outside noise, meaning you can still hear the world around you pretty clearly. Because they go in your ear somewhat, they have better sound quality and automated noise-canceling than nearly all of my top picks. Fit, however, is a big issue. While nearly all of my top picks are excellent workout companions, the AirPods 5 are quite the opposite—they can fall out often during exercise and are tricky to control mid-run.

Nothing Ear (Open) for $99: Former WIRED editor Adrienne So loves these earbuds and uses them for almost all her outdoor activities. They are very slim (19-mm wide) and lightweight. They fit slightly inside your ear and, like most Nothing headphones, have a distinct transparent design. The case doesn’t clamp shut very firmly, so be careful, but at this price, that’s forgivable.

CMF Clip Pro for $99: If you want something a little nicer and a little more comfortable than our budget pick (the Soundpeats PearClip Pro), these CMF open earbuds are a solid option. Sure, they’re a big step up in price, but for under $100, I found them more comfortable and easier to use thanks to the physical button control on each earbud (similar to my top picks).

JLab Epic Open Sport for $100: JLab’s Epic Open Sport stands out for its controls: the JLab emblem is a touch sensor, and each earbud also has physical buttons on top, making it a breeze to control. What holds the Epic Open Sport back is audio quality—the sound isn’t very detailed and is generally boring to listen to.

EarFun Clip for $70: For around $70, these are a solid pick for anyone on a budget. Former WIRED reviewer Waniata found the overall package punches above its price point, with a snappy, compact case, a comfortable fit, push-button controls, and reliable multi-device Bluetooth connections. The problem is our budget pick, the Soundpeats PearlClip Pro, often sells for less and offers a similar overall experience. Don’t dismiss these earbuds if you find them heavily discounted.

Shokz OpenFit Pro for $250: The Shokz OpenFit Pro has a great premise: open earbuds with noise canceling. Hear the world around you as you would without headphones, and turn on noise-canceling when you don’t, just like regular earbuds. And while they do cancel some noise, it’s not much. The bigger issue is price: for $250, you could get open earbuds and great noise-canceling earbuds that do a better job for each task.

Suunto Wing 2 for $179: These open-ear headphones are designed for endurance athletes—they have a long 12-hour battery life and come with a wireless power bank you can take on the go. Dock the headphones in the power bank for up to 36 hours of total battery life. There’s nothing inherently wrong with these headphones, but I don’t see a world where I would recommend them over the Shokz Openrun Pro 2, which retails for the same amount and offers more comfort, a sleeker design, better sound, and a more robust app.

Suunto Spark for $179: The Suunto Spark has a similar story to the Suunto Wing 2: it's built for athletes and syncs with Suunto fitness watches, but the similarly priced Shokz Openfit 2 outclasses it in the areas that matter (comfort, sound quality, battery).

What Are Open Earbuds and How Do They Sound?

At a glance, open earbuds seem a lot like typical wireless earbuds. The big difference is that, instead of going inside your ear canal, they rest somewhere on the outside of your ear, leaving it open so you can easily hear the world around you. I use them all the time for outdoor exercise like running or biking, where I want my music but also need to hear other runners, bikers, and cars to stay safe.

Because they leave your ears open and the sound comes from farther away (outside your ear rather than inside), audio performance isn’t as clear, intimate, or bassy as regular earbuds. They also typically lack noise-canceling features, so they are not a great fit for the office or air travel.

Open earbuds are perfect for outdoor activities or when you want to talk to other people without taking off your headphones. When I run or go for a bike ride, I only use open earbuds so I can hear my surroundings and stay safe. I also like using them around the house when I am cooking or doing laundry.

Semi-open earbuds, like Apple’s AirPods, sit inside the ear but don’t have eartips that create a sound seal. They can offer better audio quality than other open earbuds because the speakers and microphones are closer to your ear and mouth. Semi-open earbuds are comfortable, but they’re usually pretty loose—they can fall out easily and may be a poor option for fitness activities.

I test open earbuds like I test any pair of headphones: in the real world, in my daily life. I wear each pair for at least a few weeks to test comfort, battery life, ease of use, and onboard controls. I listen to podcasts, watch TV shows, jam out to my favorite playlists, and call friends.

With open earbuds in particular, I also wear them on jogs, bike rides, and errand runs to get a better feel for how stable they are on my ears. I don’t expect them to cancel any noise, so I don’t test them on airplanes or loud commutes—they aren’t really made for that. My main audio source is Spotify Lossless, but, when possible, I test other formats and codecs as well.

#462. Skydance outlines its $6 billion in planned cost savings. Here's the breakdown

Skydance is pulling back the curtain on how it plans to achieve at least $6 billion in cost savings in the next three years.

"By combining two global streaming services, two film and TV studio groups, two television portfolios, and overlapping corporate functions, we can reduce duplicative spend across an expense base of more than $50 billion," the company wrote in a letter to shareholders Monday as part of Paramount Skydance's third-quarter earnings release.

Skydance, the company formed by the combination of Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, said it projects at least $2.2 billion in cost reductions from eliminating "redundant back-office and corporate functions," including eliminating management layers and ramping the use of artificial intelligence. Those savings also account for "rationalizing" its office footprint.

Skydance expects at least $1 billion in savings from streamlining marketing and promotional spending. And around $1.4 billion in savings will come from unifying its streaming businesses and broadcast architecture and integrating back-end technology.

A final $1.4 billion in savings will be tied to "optimizing third-party spend," eliminating overlapping expenses, and consolidating purchasing decisions to one centralized group, Skydance said.

"We are confident in achieving these savings, drawing on our playbook from Paramount," the company wrote. "That program is on track to achieve over $2.7 billion in run-rate efficiencies by the end of 2026."

Some of that cost-cutting has already begun.

Last month, Skydance began offering voluntary buyouts to employees ahead of official layoffs. At the same time, the company adopted a five-day-per-week in-office policy. Additionally, last week saw the departure of eight executives — four from Warner Bros. and four from Paramount.

The company has previously said it plans to eventually merge the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services into a single platform.

On Monday, Skydance said it would take the first step toward that goal early next year, offering a bundle of the streaming services "at an even greater value."

Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz told investors during the company's earnings call that Skydance is also looking for ways to optimize its content budget. The company spends more than $30 billion annually on shows and films.

"As one company, we can make smarter choices about how we distribute and monetize both new content and library content," Kreiz said.

Skydance noted that it's expecting a $200 million headwind in the fourth quarter from theatrical underperformances at Warner Bros. studio related to already launched titles and upcoming releases.

The company will also utilize AI to speed up production and decrease costs to make films, co-CEO and chairman David Ellison said on the call.

"We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budget more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk," Ellison said.

The updates came alongside third-quarter results for the legacy Paramount Skydance company that included revenue of $7.03 billion, which was roughly in line with StreetAccount estimates of $7.04 billion, and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $1 billion, topping StreetAccount estimates of $913.5 million.

However, adjusted earnings per share of 45 cents were not immediately comparable to StreetAccount EPS estimates.

The results don't include the impact of the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, which closed after the end of the third quarter.

Skydance detailed business units of the combined entity, including studios, direct-to-consumer and TV, and said it will provide full-year guidance during its fourth-quarter earnings report early next year.

"Going forward, we will provide guidance on an annual basis, as we prioritize long-term value creation, which we believe will be more consistent with our strategic approach," the company told shareholders. "We will continue to provide quarterly updates, including certain forward-looking commentary where applicable, which will give investors visibility into our near-term progress of the business."

#457. Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO: Successor, reactions, live updates

Jensen Huang officially resigned as Nvidia CEO on Thursday morning, a seismic event in the tech industry.

Nvidia is one of the most valuable companies in the world, with a nearly $4 trillion valuation, and Huang was the company's original CEO and co-founder. The company's board of directors shared a CEO transition plan on its website, saying Huang has also resigned as president and as a member of the board, effective immediately.

The company's CFO, Colette Kress, will serve as interim CEO, while also maintaining her CFO responsibilities. The board has begun searching for a new CEO, looking both inside and outside the company.

The tech industry has been closely watching Nvidia and its stock ever since news first broke by the New York Times that Huang had not one, but two affairs, including one with an accused Chinese spy. Nvidia chips provide much of the compute that powers the fast-growing AI industry, and so Huang's alleged entanglement with a possible Chinese spy wasn't just a typical executive PR scandal, but a national security issue.

The story is still developing, so keep checking back at Mashable for the latest Nvidia news and live updates.

Last Friday, New York Times and Financial Times reported dropped a bomb on the tech industry. The publications reported that Huang had an affair with a woman the U.S. intelligence community believed to be a Chinese spy. China disputed these accusations, of course, but the Times reported that Secretary of State Marco Rubio personally wrote a memo alerting President Donald Trump to the situation in the spring.

On Nov. 8, Huang posted a personal message to the Nvidia website, admitting to and apologizing for the affair.

"I owe my family and all of you an apology," Huang wrote. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry."

It was unclear whether Huang would be able to hold on to his position as CEO, and the Nvidia board of directors announced it had opened an investigation into the incident.

But on Wednesday, news of a second affair may have sealed Huang's fate. The New York Times reported that Huang had another extramarital affair with another Nvidia executive who reported to him. The Times identified that executive as Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare.

#456. How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."

#454. Synchron Pauses Trials of Its Brain Implant Due to Performance Issues

Brain implant company Synchron has paused clinical trials of its second-generation device after it failed to perform as expected in initial study participants, WIRED has learned.

The Brooklyn-based startup, a competitor to Elon Musk’s Neuralink, is also undergoing a restructuring, which includes eliminating jobs and scaling back its footprint in New York while building up a team in San Diego to support the development of an entirely new device.

Synchron is one of several companies developing brain-computer interface technology meant to allow people to control digital devices by reading brain signals and translating those signals into output commands. The company has touted its technology, dubbed the Stentrode, as minimally invasive since it does not rest in the brain itself or require brain surgery.

Instead, the stent-like mesh tube is inserted into the jugular vein at the base of the neck, then guided with a catheter to sit on top of the brain’s motor cortex. The company’s first-generation Stentrode connected using an external wire resting on a user’s chest. Ten volunteers across the US and Australia received that device and were able to send emails, text, browse the internet, and do online shopping and banking with their thoughts. Synchron has also enabled volunteers to control an Amazon Alexa and Apple’s iPad and Vision Pro without using their hands or voice.

Synchron’s second-generation device has fewer electrodes and is fully wireless, using Bluetooth to connect. The company launched a multisite trial in the US earlier this spring with sister sites in Australia and Canada to evaluate the newer Stentrode, and some participants have already had it implanted. Those sites have recently paused enrollment, however, because the device is not working as intended, according to sources familiar with the trials who asked to remain anonymous.

In a statement to WIRED, Synchron says it voluntarily paused enrollment “after identifying a performance issue involving the lead, the cable that carries signals from the Stentrode to the implanted electronics.”

“The issue does not involve the Stentrode itself and no safety issues related to the device have been identified. The pause was a company decision, not an FDA-directed action,” the statement continues.

The company says it has since brought lead design and manufacturing in-house. Instead of fixing the lead alone, Synchron says it is upgrading the whole Stentrode system and will resume trial enrollment once the hardware upgrades are complete.

“We will continue to support participants with Stentrode devices across their lifetime. Our commitment to their care and well-being remains unchanged,” Synchron says.

Two of the five US trial sites that WIRED was able to reach confirmed that recruitment is on hold at those locations. A coordinator at one site said the pause was not safety-related and was due to Synchron taking a look at the device to make sure the company is getting the readouts it is looking for.

These trials are key to Synchron advancing the Stentrode into a larger pivotal trial, which is needed for the US Food and Drug Administration to grant marketing approval.

“We still have strong confidence that it has the potential to be a first-line therapy,” CEO Tom Oxley says of the Stentrode.

Synchron’s website and LinkedIn page show around a dozen job openings in San Diego, including electrical and mechanical engineers, microchip designers, and materials scientists. The San Diego team will be designing and manufacturing the upgraded lead and Stentrode, as well as a separate device that the company has not yet revealed that will go “beyond the blood vessels altogether,” according to its website. Oxley previously told WIRED that the company was planning to develop a device that reaches multiple brain regions at once, which could allow more complex and natural functions beyond moving a cursor or making a mouse click.

The brain-computer interface field is expanding rapidly, with money pouring into companies pursuing both implanted devices and wearables. Synchron was estimated to have a nearly $1 billion valuation after closing a $200 million funding round last fall. It is backed by Khosla Ventures and the venture firms of Bill Gates and Jeff Bezos.

Neuralink was reportedly valued as high as $42 billion in secondary market transactions as of this summer. The company had an early problem with its implant in 2024 after it malfunctioned in its first volunteer, Noland Arbaugh. The coin-sized device sits in the skull and has 64 tiny, flexible wires that are threaded into the brain. A few weeks after Arbaugh received the device, several of the wires came out of his brain, leading to a loss of cursor control. Neuralink was able to compensate for the lost threads and restore control by modifying its recording algorithm.

Recent advances in artificial intelligence are fueling the development of brain-computer interfaces since AI models can read brain patterns faster and more accurately than traditional algorithms. Merge Labs, a brain-computer interface company cofounded by OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, came out of stealth in January with $252 million in funding. OpenAI provided some of the funding, and the two companies are collaborating on AI models.

While these devices have shown incredible promise for people with severe motor disorders, they remain experimental. Synchron’s setback shows just how far the field still has to go before these systems are commercially available for patients.

#453. Everything Trump has said about Jensen Huangs Nvidia saga

The company that makes the chips behind much of the AI we use every day is suddenly looking for a new CEO.

Jensen Huang, who co-founded Nvidia and ran it for 33 years, resigned Thursday after reports of two extramarital affairs: one was reportedly with a Nvidia executive who worked for him named Kimberly Powell, the other was with Alice Huang (no relation), a former executive at Nvidia customer Megaspeed whom U.S. officials suspect of spying for Beijing.

Before all this, Trump and Huang knew each other pretty well. Huang was already one of Trump's tech advisers, and Nvidia had been asking the administration to let it sell more AI chips in China. When Trump invited Huang to Beijing at the last minute in May, the executive hopped aboard Air Force One during a fuel stop in Alaska.

When the allegations involving Alice Huang came up at Mar-a-Lago on Oct. 31, reporters asked whether Trump would keep Huang on as a tech adviser. Trump said he'd like to. "I’ve always found Jensen Huang to be very above board," he told reporters at his Florida resort on Halloween.

A little over a week later, on Nov. 8, reporters asked Trump again about Jensen Huang's future, this time at Nvidia. Earlier that day, Huang had apologized to employees in a bombshell email admitting he'd been unfaithful to his wife. Nvidia opened an investigation into whether he'd been targeted by foreign intelligence after reports of an affair with suspected spy Alice Huang. The board said at the time it had no reason to believe company secrets or U.S. national security had been compromised. Trump agreed, called Huang "an honorable guy." Did he think Huang should still run Nvidia? "I think so, oh yeah, sure."

Trump said he hadn't spoken to Huang about the allegations when he made those remarks. When he spoke to reporters again on Monday, Nov. 9, he said he'd asked Huang whether he knew about Alice Huang's alleged ties to Chinese intelligence. Jensen Huang told the president he had no idea, according to Trump. "I really believe him," the president said. #451. Rivian CEO says new R2 SUV, autonomy make 2027 an ‘inflection year’ as EV maker marks five years since IPO

It's been five years since Rivian Automotive celebrated its initial public offering with CEO and founder RJ Scaringe ringing the Nasdaq opening bell from the company's factory in Normal, Illinois, while the company's sleek vehicles sat outside the stock exchange in Times Square.

It was a euphoric time for all-electric vehicle companies that has since diminished. And much like the EV hype, Rivian's stock price is down more than 80% since the Amazon-backed company debuted on the Nasdaq at $78 per share on Nov. 10, 2021. That includes a nearly 30% decline this year.

A host of internal and external factors have contributed to Rivian's stock decline, but the company continues to be the most promising EV maker to emerge during the early 2020s, as most of its peer group to go public during that time no longer exists.

Rivian founder and CEO RJ Scaringe acknowledges that not everything has gone to plan amid supply chain shortages, geopolitical tensions and slower-than-expected consumer EV adoption, but he said he believes the next year will prove to be a catalyst for the company and investors.

"2027 is really an inflection year for us," Scaringe told CNBC during an exclusive interview. "I'm excited to start to really show what we believe so fully, the potential of the business."

Scaringe's bullishness comes as Rivian ramps up production of its midsize R2 SUV — meant to expand the brand to the mainstream market — and as the company continues to invest in its next generation of vehicle software and autonomy.

Scaringe said the R2, with an entry-level model at about $45,000, remains the core business to be able to showcase its newest technologies. Rivian delivered a record 19,248 vehicles in the third quarter of 2026, up 46% year over year, and said R2 was already its best-selling vehicle by volume, outselling both its R1 flagship products and its commercial van.

"That's going to define the business. The path to profitability, the path to very significant growth in revenue is all built around R2, and then some of the variants that come following R2," Scaringe said.

For the most recent period, Rivian said the automotive cost of goods sold per vehicle improved by more than $12,000 quarter over quarter, helped by a higher R2 mix and better fixed-cost leverage.

Rivian also is in the midst of building a new multibillion-dollar Georgia factory that Scaringe has said is necessary to get the company to the volumes it needs to achieve net profitability.

Its sole factory in Normal, Illinois, is capable of producing 215,000 vehicles annually. That includes up to 155,000 R2s alongside R1 models and electric vans. Rivian said the Georgia plant will be capable of producing up to 300,000 units annually once the initial phase construction is completed in late 2028.

Of course, all of that means additional capital spending and pressure on the company to improve profits.

"We've made the decision to simultaneously work towards profitability … and deeply invest in our technology, and in particular in autonomy," Scaringe said.

Rivian's operating performance is improving, but several Wall Street analysts expect the company's investment requirements to rise significantly in 2027.

Rivian generated $176 million of consolidated gross profit in the third quarter, but its automotive business posted an $80 million gross loss, while its software and services unit generated $256 million of gross profit at a 42% margin.

Wall Street analysts broadly agree Rivian's R2 launch is showing encouraging early demand and lowering manufacturing costs, but they're concerned about whether that can offset rising autonomy spending, the Georgia factory buildout and continued cash burn.

"Funding has long been a key debate for Rivian investors," Deutsche Bank analyst Edison Yu said in an Oct. 30 investor note after the company reported quarterly results that exceeded Wall Street's expectations. "The notion of a multi-year (even if credible) path to cash flow breakeven will be less attractive to investors."

Rivian said during its late October earnings call that its total available future liquidity is expected to be about $13 billion when factoring in available credit and money from partnerships with Volkswagen and Uber as well as a $4.5 billion U.S. Department of Energy loan for the Georgia plant.

Rivian said its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments balance was $5.5 billion to end the third quarter.

The company may still need to raise more capital. Scaringe said Rivian plans to be "opportunistic" about any additional capital raises, which have previously included equity deals with Volkswagen and Uber.

"As we think about the environment looking out, we want to continue to position ourselves to be in a place to invest in the technology. And so that means we'll be opportunistic as it pertains to capital. But I want to be clear: We're like very focused on getting the profitability," Scaringe said.

The company has significantly upgraded the software capabilities and hardware on its vehicles to prepare them for autonomy technologies, or physical artificial intelligence, as Scaringe calls it.

The next step in that is releasing "point-to-point" driving capabilities, in which the vehicles can drive on their own from destination to destination with the driver acting as a monitor in case of any unexpected problems.

U.S. EV leader Tesla is the only automaker to currently offer those features on its vehicles. Scaringe said Rivian plans to not only catch up to Tesla's capabilities but exceed them as it uses multiple sensor types to allow the vehicle to "see" better, especially in inclement weather.

"That's not like the finish line. ... Think of it as like the starting point where you suddenly have a more apples to apples comparison between Rivian and Tesla," Scaringe said.

The release of point-to-point over the coming weeks and months, first to employees and then to customers, is expected to be followed by autonomous vehicles with Uber by 2028.

Scaringe said he believes such technologies will become a differentiator for the company in the coming years.

"I think it will go from a purchase consideration for maybe 10% of the buying public that's in the top five things considered in making a purchase decision to the No. 1 or No. 2 purchase consideration for the vast majority of buyers," Scaringe said.

Rivian plans to offer more details about its AI and autonomous plans during a second annual investor technology event on Dec. 9.

"We view this as a likely positive catalyst as the event could set a path for AV features driving incremental vehicle demand & higher [software] revenue in 2027, building on 2026 growth," TD Cowen analyst Itay Michaeli said in an investor note last week.

Rivian's stock is currently rated overweight with a $17.70 target price, according to analyst estimates compiled by FactSet. That would be a roughly 26% increase from its Monday closing price but still a far cry from its IPO.

#450. Nvidia chaos, Dem sweep, lawsuits, protests: AIs terrible, no-good November

Investors and industry experts have long worried that the AI industry will expand until it inevitably bursts. And in the wake of a historic midterm election cycle that decidedly challenged the interests of Big Tech, the AI industry is facing enormous strain from all sides.

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, a leading figure of the AI boom, has gone down in a salacious scandal involving extramarital affairs and accusations of Chinese espionage. SpaceXSI owner Elon Musk invested and lost millions on Republican campaigns during the midterms that he and other Big Tech leaders hoped would open up the floodgates to greater AI infrastructure. OpenAI and Anthropic have been implicated in numerous cybersecurity scandals on top of a domestic mental health crisis. Google, Meta, and OpenAI's ChatGPT are at the epicenter of a child safety reckoning that could change education and social media forever.

The latest challenge the industry is facing down is a lawsuit filed today (Nov. 12) by a safety and civil society groups Public Citizen, Irreplaceable Education Fund, Heat Initiative, and The Alliance for Secure AI. The groups argue that AI's developers are aware of "imminent and catastrophic risks to humanity" posed by AI and urges courts to freeze the industry's development completely in the name of public safety.

"Despite endless warnings about the grave risks their products pose to public safety and even civilization itself, AI company executives are rushing to develop and deploy ever-more advanced AI systems," wrote Robert Weissman, co-president of Public Citizen, in a press release. "We the People should not simply stand by and tolerate AI executives’ unhinged risk-taking at the public’s expense. Our lawsuit asks the court to apply existing law and find the AI corporations’ reckless practices unlawful and freeze further AI development."

The group argues that companies are risking public safety by developing and releasing products they have admitted are potentially harmful, and that further development can be halted under California's Unfair Competition Law. The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman have previously agreed on the need to slow the growth of highly advanced AI systems. In a September essay, Amodei proposed a three-point plan to "pace the frontier" of AI, which Altman later signaled his support for.

"Children have already paid the price for Big Tech prioritizing profit over safety. We’ve watched chatbots push kids toward suicide and AI tools turn their ordinary photos into AI-generated child sexual abuse material,” said Brooke Istook, CEO of child advocacy organization Heat Initiative. "Now these companies are racing to build AI systems they admit they may not be able to control. If that happens, the protections we’ve spent years fighting for won’t be enough."

Some believe the country's incoming Democratic Congress may finally hold tech companies' feet to the fire. Coinciding with the lawsuit, dozens of survivor parent-advocates sent a letter to California Attorney General Rob Bonta and Delaware Attorney General Kathy Jennings, urging them to compel OpenAI to restructure its corporate structure and board membership to better support safe AI deployment. The group argues the two states, which led a joint investigation into OpenAI's recent restructuring, could force the AI company into adopting even stronger safety measures.

While the industry debates appropriate regulation and courts mull over the legal system's power over AI, schools are becoming their own battleground.

In the first education-related lawsuit of its kind, a family is arguing their teen's access to AI on school-issued Chromebooks posed a life-threatening risk to her mental health.

Then on Nov. 11, a coalition of school and child safety groups led nationwide protests over Google's new controversy-stirring policies for schools. In an announcement a week prior, Google announced that its AI tools would now be opt-in by default, including its AI-powered Search. Because Search's AI tools can't be turned off, districts with AI bans would be forced to choose between allowing Search or violating AI guidelines.

Advocates argue that Google's "ultimatum" presents an opportunity to pressure the tech giant into relinquishing some of its far-reaching AI goals. Many have urged districts to cancel contracts with the company and find AI-free alternatives.

#448. Fossil CEO says turnaround is ahead of plan as sales return to growth

Fossil Group posted quarterly results on Tuesday that could point to early signs of a turnaround for the legacy watchmaker.

The company, which has struggled to compete with new gadgets in the years since its sales and stock price peaked in the 2010s, has been quietly undertaking a comeback strategy under CEO Franco Fogliato. The company's fiscal third-quarter earnings indicate the strategy is beginning to bear fruit.

The company said Tuesday that it returned to profitability in the third quarter, posting operating income of $25.3 million, compared with an operating loss of $21.7 million last year. It also returned to sales growth, with net sales up 0.4% to $271.2 million. The company last reported a year-over-year sales increase in the first fiscal quarter of 2022.

"We're excited about improving dramatically the profitability of the company through a very strong and disciplined management of full-price sales as well as cost management," Fogliato told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

For the quarter ended Oct. 3, Fossil reported net income of $11.7 million, or 19 cents per share, compared with a net loss of $39.9 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior. The company also said its operating expenses fell 11.1% to $137 million. The company last posted quarterly net income in the third fiscal quarter of 2022.

With the signs of growth, the retailer also raised its full-year outlook, guiding for full-year net sales of between $980 million and $990 million, which would mark a slight decline from just over $1 billion in net sales in the prior year. That compares with a previous expectation of net sales falling between 3% and 5%. Fossil also said it's expecting positive free cash flow by the end of the year.

Fogliato said Fossil had previously not expected sales growth for another year and a half.

The stronger quarter comes after the company saw slumping sales and financial losses over the past few years. It struggled with the rise of smartwatches, mounting debt and the decline in mall foot traffic.

Fossil stock has lost the vast majority of its value since the early-to-mid 2010s, and despite a recent surge, was trading under $8 a share on Tuesday.

To rightsize the business, Fogliato, who joined Fossil in 2024, said he focused on closing stores, moving away from promotions to full-price sales, improving marketing and broadening Fossil's product range. That meant shrinking the business to a sustainable baseline before trying to grow it.

The company saw a 5.9% decrease in direct-to-consumer sales for its third quarter, which Fogliato attributed to the company's "aggressive" downsizing.

"We simplified a lot of the business doing this," Fogliato said, adding that customers responded well when the company moved away from markdowns. "People tell me they are excited about what they are seeing in the marketplace now."

Fossil added that its key growth engine for the quarter was Asia, where net sales grew 15.1%. Fogliato said much of that growth was led by the India market.

At the same time, growing internationally is only part of the watchmaker's strategy as it tries to improve its performance in its home country.

"Priority number one is to grow our home market, and we're doing that," he said. "As retail is settling into a smaller, more profitable base, ready to grow again, we're entering 2027 with plenty of opportunities."

Fogliato said one key to growth was not trying to keep up with the smartwatch trend. While trying to make gains in the smartwatch space initially hampered Fossil's performance, it's now investing in analog watches again.

Smartwatch sales came in under $1 million for the quarter, but sales for traditional watches — which make up around 86% of Fossil's sales — grew 6.4%.

"Fossil is literally having a comeback, a strong comeback, a momentum, and we can see this," Fogliato said. "And it comes not only through the numbers — it comes with the excitement we see in the stores, and we think we're just at the beginning. We have so much work to do."

#447. Jensen Huang Basically Lit More Than $1 Trillion on Fire in Less Than a Month

On Thursday morning, Nvidia announced that Jensen Huang had resigned from the company that had become, in many ways, synonymous with its chief executive.

Less than an hour later, when the markets opened, Nvidia’s stock had lost roughly $546 billion in market value. Though it has rebounded somewhat, the last weeks of scandal—including a reported second affair with an Nvidia employee—have tanked the chipmaker’s valuation by more than $1 trillion.

Huang’s very public downfall began in late October, when The New York Times reported that the CEO had a “close relationship” with Alice Huang, a former executive at the Singapore-based chip buyer Megaspeed and an alleged asset of China’s Ministry of State Security. (The two are not known to be related.) At that time, Nvidia’s market cap was around $5.2 trillion.

On November 8, Nvidia announced it was investigating its CEO, after Jensen publicly acknowledged the affair. By the following morning, roughly $1 trillion had been erased since the scandal started.

If you hang out with a certain type of person who thinks of themselves as a daytrader, they might have talked about “buying the dip” over drinks last weekend. If you believe Nvidia can thrive without Huang, there’s still time! The dip has dipped even further. But the early consensus seems to be that this could be more of a prolonged plunge.

“Huang is irreplaceable,” Ryan Fedasiuk, a senior fellow at the think tank Foundation for American Innovation, told WIRED last week. “He built Nvidia into the juggernaut it is today, and he was the visionary behind the GPU, which has sustained the intelligence revolution.”

There are very real questions about what the end of Huang means for the global economy. Not only are Nvidia’s chips used to train most major AI models, but the company has also invested billions in a number of other tech companies. As of an August regulatory filing, Nvidia owned nearly $30 billion worth of Intel stock and roughly $21 billion of SpaceX stock.

For now, Nvidia’s loss is other chipmakers’ gain. AMD shares are up 28 percent over the past five days, and Broadcom is up nearly 16 percent. Nothing to see here.

#444. What is Microsoft Exchange? The cloud tool behind Microsoft Outlook issues.

Microsoft's popular suite of enterprise products, known as Microsoft 365, which includes Outlook, Teams, and Copilot, suffered from a massive outage on Thursday.

While more than 100,000 users reported problems accessing Microsoft Outlook and Microsoft 365 on the platform DownDetector, Microsoft is saying it's actually a cloud tool called Microsoft Exchange that's causing problems.

So, what is Microsoft Exchange? And why does it being down affect your ability to access your email or message your coworkers? The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Microsoft Exchange is the backend cloud tool that sends data updates to Outlook. It's essentially the server that stores the actual data, handles the mail flow, syncs the data to services like Outlook, and controls account security policies. Put simply, Outlook is the front end; Exchange is the back end.

For most users, though, this is a distinction without a difference. The end result is that users can't access their email, calendar, or messages.

Mashable reached out to Microsoft with questions about the outage, but the company has not responded, as of this writing.

#443. Google DeepMind Staff Tap the UK Government to Escalate Their Unionization Fight

Google DeepMind employees in the UK have asked the government to intervene in a push to unionize the artificial intelligence lab.

On Thursday, the Communication Workers Union and Unite the Union—put forward by Google DeepMind staff as joint representatives—filed an application with the Central Arbitration Committee, a government body that resolves disputes between employers and unions.

The application, which was seen by WIRED, asks the CAC to confirm that the unions have enough support from workers to trigger a unionization ballot. The company would be required by law to respect the results. If successful, roughly 1,000 staff working in product-focused roles at Google DeepMind would qualify for union membership.

The escalation follows a series of meetings between employees, the unions, and company representatives over voluntary unionization, which left staff with the impression that their demands weren’t being taken seriously.

“They’ve been ignored. They feel like their problems have been minimized,” John Chadfield, national officer for technology at the CWU, tells WIRED. “Management has refused to come to the table. They sent it to the HR and labor relations people, who dealt with it as an administrative box-ticking exercise.”

“We respect the right of employees to make an informed decision about whether or not to join a union,” Google DeepMind spokesperson Tom Hewett tells WIRED in a statement. “We have consistently listened to both those seeking to unionize and those opposed, and have acted in good faith from the outset. We remain committed to constructively participating in this process, and have maintained open and transparent communication with our employees and the unions throughout.”

The push to unionize began in February 2025, when Alphabet, Google’s parent company, removed a pledge from its ethics guidelines stating that it wouldn’t use AI for purposes like weapons development.

“When I joined, we really made a big deal about the principles—the red lines. We were really looking to benefit humanity and make the world a better place,” a Google DeepMind employee, who asked to remain anonymous for fear of backlash, tells WIRED. “A lot of this is about reestablishing that ethical and moral framework that we started with.”

The employees pushing for unionization object in particular to agreements between the company and the US and Israeli militaries. If they succeed in unionizing, they intend to demand that Google backs out of those contracts.

“We feel extremely concerned about the direction that this technology is going in,” says a second Google DeepMind employee, who asked to remain anonymous for the same reason as the first employee.

The company has previously defended its deals with government organizations.

“We are proud to be part of a broad consortium of leading AI labs and technology and cloud companies providing AI services and infrastructure in support of national security,” Jenn Crider, a Google spokeswoman, told The New York Times in April. “We remain committed to the private and public sector consensus that AI should not be used for domestic mass surveillance or autonomous weaponry without appropriate human oversight.”

In an email delivered to all UK staff on Thursday, seen by WIRED, the Google DeepMind employees involved in the unionization push appealed to their colleagues to participate in the ballot, which they expect to take place in the coming months. “We want a union, we want collective bargaining, and we want a say in how AI is developed and deployed,” they wrote.

The group also hopes it can establish a playbook that employees at other major AI labs like OpenAI and Anthropic can follow in the UK.

“It’s pretty rare that there’s been an industry that exercises such dominance and power over the economy, where there’s so little opportunity for employees or regulators to have any checks and balances,” the second Google DeepMind employee says. “We want to show what can happen when employees collectively organize and articulate a shared vision for the way their technology should be used.”

#441. Microsoft Outlook outage cause seemingly revealed

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 10:58 p.m. ET / 7:58 p.m. PT — Unfortunately, Microsoft's efforts to bring servers back online aren't going as planned. It appears to have been unable to address the issue of them becoming unresponsive again, and has stated it is now searching for different ways to increase traffic capacity.

"We've monitored the status of the changes applied to improve serviceability and confirmed they're not taking as expected," Microsoft wrote in an update on X.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 8:07 p.m. ET / 5:07 p.m. PT — Microsoft has revised its list of features and services impacted by this outage, stating that it is affecting ones that interact with Exchange Online and email services. Microsoft Outlook, SharePoint Online, Teams, Copilot AI, and 365 are all continuing to have various issues, though Azure appears to be operational.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 7:53 p.m. ET / 4:53 p.m. PT — Microsoft has issued a new update stating that the servers they've brought back online may become unresponsive again when they receive traffic. The company is currently investigating the issue, though in the meantime it's limiting the types of traffic going to these servers in some locations.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 6:23 p.m. ET / 3:23 p.m. PT — Microsoft states that it is bringing some servers back online, and "they remain stable so far." Users should expect a "gradual recovery."

"We're also shifting traffic to alternate routes wherever possible and observing limited recovery," Microsoft wrote in an update on X. "In parallel, we've streamlined the process of returning the remaining affected servers to service."

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 5:26 p.m. ET / 2:26 p.m. PT — Microsoft services remained down on Thursday evening, as Microsoft acknowledged ongoing problems in an update posted to X.

The Microsoft global outage has affected Outlook, Teams, and other Microsoft 365 services, starting around 12:30 p.m. ET. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

If Outlook wouldn't load for you on Thursday, Microsoft now has a theory about what went wrong. The company said in an X post on Nov. 12 that a component responsible for checking whether its servers are working properly may be behind the failures that took some of them out of service.

In its service health update, Microsoft said some servers went offline after failing automated health checks. With parts of its infrastructure under heavier load than usual across multiple regions, the company said that extra strain was likely contributing to connection problems.

Microsoft also says it’s "deploying a targeted change to bypass this component on a subset of servers" to see if that brings them back online. It has also taken steps to keep more servers from going out of service. So far, Microsoft says it hasn’t found a recent change that coincided with the start of the outage. The company is still investigating why the checks failed.

Microsoft initially flagged trouble accessing Exchange Online mailboxes in the Americas. Exchange Online is the service that stores and delivers email for many Outlook users.

The company later warned that the issue "may affect Microsoft 365 service connectivity for users in any region." Around 1 p.m. ET, nearly 130,000 Outlook problem reports appeared on Downdetector, and users reported trouble loading their inboxes and sending or receiving emails. Copilot users also reported problems.

Earlier Thursday, Microsoft had also flagged problems with Teams in North America under incident TM1550488. In a follow-up on X, the company said its automated recovery systems had kicked in and were reducing the disruption.

Microsoft is posting updates under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center. If the admin center is also out of reach, Microsoft is directing users to its public service status page.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 4:17 p.m. ET / 1:17 p.m. PT — In its latest update, Microsoft stated on X that it's deploying fixes and some servers have already come back online. The company's status page still listed Microsoft 365 service as "degraded."

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 3:01 p.m. ET / 12:01 p.m. PT — The Microsoft 365 Status account on X shared updates explaining why the outage is happening and what they are doing to fix it.

If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Thousands of Microsoft Outlook users reported an outage on Thursday afternoon, with user error reports for Microsoft products spiking sharply.

The platform DownDetector showed error reports spiking around 12:30 p.m. ET. By 1:12 p.m. ET, the service had received more than 130,000 reports, indicating serious problems with the popular email service. Virtually all Microsoft services showed elevated error reports, including Microsoft 365, Microsoft Copilot, and Microsoft Teams.

Mashable reached out to Microsoft for comment, and we'll update this story if we receive more information.

From the beginning, there were signs of a widespread problem with Microsoft services. Case in point: The Microsoft service status page was not loading for Mashable reporters for part of the day, though it has since come back online.

The Microsoft 365 Status account on X initially posted a message acknowledging an outage at 12:54 p.m. ET: "We're investigating reports of users experiencing issues accessing Exchange Online mailboxes through multiple connection methods in the Americas."

At 1:07 p.m. ET, the company wrote, "While we're working to mitigate impact, we've confirmed the issue may affect Microsoft 365 service connectivity for users in any region, more information will be provided in the Microsoft 365 admin center under EX1550543." The company added, "a portion of infrastructure is experiencing elevated load across multiple regions, which is likely contributing to the connectivity errors."

The company's status page indicated that the outage specifically affects Microsoft 365, the company's suite of tools for enterprise customers. Microsoft consumer products should still be operational.

Microsoft also clarified that users may have trouble accessing Microsoft Teams, Microsoft Exchange Online, SharePoint Online, Microsoft Copilot 365, and Microsoft Defender XDR, in particular.

In the latest updates, posted to X at 4:06 p.m. ET, Microsoft said it had started deploying a fix that would allow some servers to come back online. The company has also found "a small number of configuration updates" that may be part of the overall problem.

Outlook previously experienced a widespread outage in August. At the time, the company cited a "misconfiguration issue that prevented authentication components from deploying correctly.

#436. Measles is surging in the U.S. — and outbreaks can cost millions

Measles outbreaks aren't just a public health threat. They can also be a financial burden on the U.S. — and the cost could only deepen as the once-eliminated disease spreads more widely.

The U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status following a review by the Pan American Health Organization this week, after cases ratcheted to recent highs this year.

More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported nationwide in 2026 as of early November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. Eight deaths associated with the disease have been confirmed by states so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The tool also estimates that 2025 measles outbreaks in the U.S. — which involved 2,289 confirmed cases nationwide – carried an economic cost of more than $37 million.

The costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources health departments spend investigating and containing outbreaks, as well as wages lost when people have to miss work. The tool uses more recent, state-specific cost and financing data than an initial study by the researchers, which relied on historical outbreak costs.

The estimates from the tool are not a definitive accounting of what the outbreaks actually cost. But it illustrates the hefty bill tied to the spread of a disease that can be prevented through vaccination, and the potential economic consequences of falling childhood immunization rates. CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases.

"We're starting to see a whole bunch of people who decided not to get vaccinated as kids or have never gotten exposed to measles because they benefited from herd immunity, who now make it into adulthood and risk potentially getting quite seriously ill and requiring very significant medical expenses, or dying at the hospital," Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, who helped develop the tool, said in an interview.

Measles outbreaks can trigger a costly public health response, even when many people who are exposed never become sick.

When a case is identified, public health officials must investigate the infection, identify and notify people who may have been exposed, coordinate with hospitals and schools, and sometimes organize vaccination campaigns to prevent further spread. Those efforts can divert staff and resources from other public health priorities.

Costs could differ heavily depending on the state.

Johns Hopkins' data includes national averages alongside state-specific inputs, including inpatient hospital costs and wages from government data or trusted health policy sources. In New York, for example, the average inpatient hospital stay is roughly $4,200 a day, while the minimum wage is $16 per hour.

The tool estimates that the 48 cases reported by New York in 2025 carried an estimated $1.1 million in costs, including nearly $749,000 in public health responses, $333,000 in productivity losses and roughly $24,500 in direct medical expenses.

Meanwhile, the 803 cases in Texas, the epicenter of the largest U.S. measles outbreak in 2025, carried estimated costs of nearly $13 million, according to the tool. That includes $9 million in public health costs, $4 million in productivity losses and around $288,000 in direct medical expenses.

The tool builds on a study published by Patenaude and other researchers in August, which examined the costs of measles outbreaks in the U.S. The researchers reviewed published studies and government reports to compile historical estimates of medical expenses and public health response costs across 18 states.

The paper estimated that outbreaks carried a historical average cost of nearly $59,000 per case, just from the public health response. The researchers also estimated how costs scale with outbreak size, separating fixed up-front response expenses from the additional costs associated with each new case.

"A lot of this is incurred up front, and that's because of the human labor that's involved in contact tracing and all of the different actors that sort of need to be mobilized to deal with the fact that there's an outbreak," Patenaude told CNBC. "It's a largely labor-driven cost."

That model estimated roughly $298,000 in fixed costs and about $16,300 for each additional case. But it relied on historical data, including a particularly large 2019 outbreak in New York City, where contact-tracing labor costs were high. The researchers also excluded productivity losses from that model because most of the historical studies they reviewed did not consistently account for them.

The economic burden of measles extends beyond the costs of treating infected patients and organizing a public health response.

People who are exposed to measles may have to quarantine, even if they never develop symptoms. Parents can also be forced to miss work to care for sick or exposed children, while workers who contract the disease may lose wages during their recovery.

A 2025 study by Joey Mattingly, a pharmacist and health economist at the University of Utah, used published epidemiological and cost data to model the potential economic burden of a hypothetical 1,500-case outbreak. His analysis included direct medical expenses, public health spending and lost productivity, and examined how the estimates changed under different assumptions, including hospitalization rates.

The study estimated that the hypothetical outbreak could cost about $90 million, including roughly $26 million in productivity losses.

Those indirect costs can add up quickly because measles is highly contagious and a single infected person can expose dozens of others. Those contacts may need to quarantine or miss work while health officials determine whether they have been infected, creating costs even when they never develop the disease.

Mattingly estimated that each person who is quarantined could amount to roughly $300 in lost productivity alone.

If a single measles case exposes 40 to 50 people who then need to quarantine, that could translate into an estimated $12,000 to $15,000 in lost productivity, even if none of those contacts become sick.

That ripple effect is one reason Mattingly argues that the economic costs of outbreaks can quickly outweigh the investment required to vaccinate people against measles.

"Even if you don't get the infection, the contact costs you," he said. "And so it's just showing that there's this multiplication or exponential growth on the economic side that happens."

The financial burden can also fall unevenly across communities. While the costs of responding to an outbreak may be shared among local, state and federal agencies, Mattingly said the impact is often felt directly by local health departments.

"It's not like your health department is sitting around waiting for measles outbreaks," he said. "They're working, doing all these other things, and imagine having to just drop what you're doing because there's been a huge outbreak in the community."

Patenaude said the findings could help state and local health departments better anticipate the resources needed to contain outbreaks — particularly as measles cases continue to rise.

"It's not super expensive to treat and deal with a single case of measles compared to a single case of cancer," he said. "But if you have these outbreaks popping up and you're having to constantly divert resources that could be spent elsewhere by state and local health departments on measles, then you're going to wind up spending a lot of money very quickly."

#433. Who could replace Jensen Huang at Nvidia long-term? Here are the top contenders.

After reports of multiple affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, Jensen Huang is out at Nvidia.

With Huang's resignation from the chipmaking giant that he helped found, who will take the helm at Nvidia?

While Nvidia quickly announced an interim CEO in Colette Kress, there's a lot of speculation about who will take a more permanent role as head of one of the world's most valuable companies.

First up is Nvidia's longtime Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress, already a billionaire in her own right.

Kress technically has the CEO job right now, as Nvidia paired Huang's resignation announcement with the news that Kress would fill in as interim CEO. And, if she's able to right the ship and help Nvidia come out of Huang's scandals relatively unscathed, she's a favorite to take the position long-term, too.

Before being promoted to the interim CEO position, Kress has worked as the CFO at the company since 2013. She previously worked as CFO for divisions at Microsoft and Cisco. Nvidia's investors are familiar with her too from the company's quarterly earnings calls, so she may have the upper hand there too.

Kress may have the advantage of being the interim CEO, but Ian Buck seems to be the favorite amongst the speculators.

Like Kress, Buck would also be an internal choice for CEO. Buck has worked at Nvidia since 2004 and is currently the company's Vice President and General Manager of Hyperscale and High-Performance Computing (HPC).

In his role at Nvidia, Buck is responsible for the company's data center, cloud, and HPC product lines — all central to Nvidia's success amid the AI boom.

In addition, Buck is credited as the creator of CUDA, or Compute Unified Device Architecture. CUDA is Nvidia's proprietary platform that developers use to run their software on Nvidia's GPUs.

Rene Haas's name has immediately been floated as a potential fit as Nvidia CEO. Why?

If investors are looking for an outsider to come in and take over Nvidia, Rene Haas is likely the guy to do it.

Haas is currently the CEO of British chipmaker Arm, so he already works as a high-level executive within the industry. And Arm and Nvidia have collaborated for over a decade on designing and manufacturing CPUs. #428. Climate Change Has Pushed the World's Ice Out of Its Safe Zone

The world is no longer safe for frozen H2O.

New research published in Science on Thursday shows that the conditions that have kept ice, frozen soil, and snow stable are slipping away due to the burning of fossil fuels—and the implications are grave for the entire globe.

The climate of the past 12,000 or so years has been remarkably steady. That’s allowed for all kinds of wonderful things to happen, like human civilization flourishing. But rising temperatures are pushing the climate outside that envelope, and the new research looks at what the team behind the new findings has called the “safe operating space” for the icy, snowy landscape known as the cryosphere.

The scientists looked at changes to polar sea and land ice, mountain glaciers, permafrost, and snow cover over the past 12 millennia to analyze conditions that have allowed both civilization and other ecosystems to thrive. They then identified boundaries on those conditions that, if crossed, would upend the stability and safety humans have enjoyed for millennia.

The results show an “accelerating and partly irreversible loss of cryospheric integrity.” Arctic and Antarctic sea ice have both fallen outside the safe operating threshold, as have snow cover and the area of permafrost—frozen soil laden with carbon. While land ice hasn’t yet fallen out of its safe space, it’s on the brink. And because of warming that’s already been locked into the climate system, it’s likely to also fall outside the threshold in the coming centuries.

While it may feel like the Antarctic ice sheet or permafrost covering Alaska are distant concerns, they’re anything but. The West Antarctic Ice Sheet, which a growing body of evidence shows is nearing the brink of irreversible decline, has enough ice to raise sea levels by 11 feet (3 meters). Permafrost, meanwhile, holds trillions of tons of carbon dioxide. Any degradation means that CO2, as well as even more potent methane, could end up in the atmosphere, accelerating global warming even further. Other examples of major risks abound, including how melting Greenland land ice could slow down or even collapse the ocean conveyor belt that helps keep Europe temperate.

“The consequences of a changing cryosphere extend beyond regions where ice and snow are disappearing,” says Albert Norström, a climate researcher at the Stockholm Resilience Center who co-authored the new paper.

Dipping outside the safe operating space doesn’t mean that the world will see 11 feet of sea level rise in the next year, a trillion-ton CO2 eruption, or a Day After Tomorrow scenario. But it does mean that the odds of crossing dangerous tipping points are rising.

“As we scratch 1.5 [degrees Celsius] and go above it, we are just really gambling with potentially triggering some of these Earth system tipping points,” Norström says. The safe operating space framework also “takes a broader perspective” that assesses slower changes over time and “how far, in this case, the cryosphere is moving away from the conditions that have supported a relatively stable Earth system,” he adds.

Cutting emissions is the most obvious and straightforward way to stop the cryosphere from sliding further outside our comfort zone. But there are a growing number of proposals to pursue more radical interventions that could protect the world’s ice. Scientists have already draped glaciers in the Alps in blankets during the summer to prevent melting. Other researchers have explored techniques to pump cold sea water to the surface to help thicken ice and modeled whether a manmade barrier could stabilize the West Antarctic Ice Sheet.

These geoengineering approaches have the potential to be costly, and there’s no guarantee they would work. They’d also do nothing to stop the root cause of warming: burning fossil fuels. Norström says emissions cuts are the most urgent way to protect ice and snow (and ourselves), but with the cryosphere sliding into a more dangerous space, “in principle, we shouldn't put the lid on” research for more far-flung interventions.

“The safe operating space is a precautionary framework,” he says. “It ensures that we try to stay as far downstream from the point where these really potentially existential or huge consequences and risks can happen.”

Update: 11/12/2026, 4:13 PM EST: WIRED corrected the name of Albert Norström.
