#378. Nvidia privately confirmed it could track chips to fight smuggling, even as industry publicly questioned feasibility

The Chip Security Act is getting renewed attention following Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s resignation and reported affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Representatives from Nvidia told Illinois representative Bill Foster that it would be technically possible to implement the anti-smuggling measures called for in the Chip Security Act, Foster told MIT Technology Review in an interview on Thursday, even while an industry group claimed they were “potentially infeasible.”

Foster, a Democrat who serves on the Committee on Science, Space, and Technology, is a cosponsor of the act, which would introduce geotracking requirements to verify the location of advanced AI chips in order to prevent their smuggling to China. It is one of three chip-security bills under consideration for inclusion in the Senate’s National Defense Authorization Act, each of which is drawing renewed attention after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s resignation and reported affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

When the Chip Security Act was introduced, in May 2025, lawmakers said the technology to implement it existed, something Nvidia has not publicly confirmed. In March 2026, the Semiconductor Industry Association, of which Nvidia is a member, explicitly opposed the bill and wrote that its mandates for chip tracking were “new, untested, and potentially infeasible.”

But privately, Nvidia representatives working with Foster told the lawmaker that the company could implement them.

“They never put anything in writing,” Foster says, but during technical discussions on the possibility of such safeguards, their reaction was “yeah, of course we can do this.” This private acknowledgment to Foster goes further than anything Nvidia has previously made public.

Other technology Nvidia has built for its customers suggests that the company can indeed offer accurate tracking of its chips’ locations. In December 2025, for example, it announced a new tool called Fleet Intelligence that would allow customers to monitor how its advanced chips were performing across different data center locations. In doing so, the tool could indicate which country chips were in, Nvidia confirmed to Reuters, but the company would not elaborate on whether this technology could be used to prevent smuggling.

Nvidia did not respond to requests for comment or confirmation of Foster’s characterization.

In early October, when lawmakers were reportedly increasingly concerned about Nvidia’s role in smuggling, MIT Technology Review asked the company if Fleet Intelligence could be used to prevent the practice. It declined to answer directly, instead sending a statement: “Governments and media have found insignificant diversion of controlled US products—a drop in the bucket compared to the ocean of domestic compute China already has.”

The recent fallout at Nvidia involves a number of people and companies alleged to be involved in chip smuggling. Alice Huang, with whom Jensen Huang reportedly had an affair, runs Megaspeed, a Singaporean firm now under investigation for smuggling advanced GPUs to China. A US Commerce Department inquiry into the company was reportedly halted after invention by Nvidia. Megaspeed reportedly bought chips from Supermicro, a company that packages Nvidia’s chips onto servers.

The Chip Security Act as drafted would require a mechanism to track the locations of chips, either by inspections of facilities, attestations by companies, or ping-based location mechanisms. The ping-based technique, the hardest option to circumvent, would require the GPU to send a signal of its location when it comes online. In September, the Center for Security and Emerging Technology found it would be the most cost-effective option.

Foster said he did not ask Nvidia’s representatives why they weren’t voluntarily adopting such chip-tracking tools if they could technically do so.

But if the requirements of the bill pass as part of the Senate’s National Defense Authorization Act, the new tracking mechanisms would only tell investigators where the chips are, not offer a way to disconnect them if it’s revealed they have been smuggled. Foster told MIT Technology Review he intends to introduce new, stricter legislation in the new year that would offer a workaround. The new rule would require licenses to operate advanced GPUs, and before a GPU could connect, its valid license would need to be verified.

In the meantime, the controversy at Nvidia, Foster says, has fueled support among his colleagues for the rules in the bill: “There will be a lot of political pressure, in the wake of everything that’s happened, on the companies to actually do a very good job of the geographic validation of where the chip actually is.”

#375. Peacock is redesigning its mobile app to focus more on vertical videos

This is an attempt to “win another hour per user, per month.”

NBCUniversal is completely overhauling the Peacock app on mobile. This redesign will further emphasize vertical video content, just like TikTok, YouTube Shorts and all the rest. Company chairman Matt Strauss recently said that this is an attempt to "win another hour per user, per month," according to The Hollywood Reporter.

"We recognize that we have to start thinking of ourselves less as a streaming platform and more of an entertainment platform," Strauss said. "How do we super serve our franchises and our fandoms where the best experience is on our app?"

Bravo fandom has always extended far beyond the episode itself. @Inc goes inside how @Peacock is building on that passion with Your Bravoverse, a new personalized experience that helps fans explore two decades of unforgettable @BravoTV moments. https://t.co/PETJdEzTsy

— NBCUniversal (@NBCUniversal) November 4, 2026

In addition to a larger emphasis on vertical video, the app is launching fandom hubs for various franchises, like the Bravo-verse or The Office. These hubs will be home to short videos and affiliated experiences. For instance, the Law & Order page will feature little video games to fool around with. The NBA page will offer "easy access to player stats" and "deeper game information sitting directly underneath the live feed in a dual screen view."

There are a couple of techy features heading to the app. Peacock will continue to experiment with live vertical video. There will soon be a "dynamic video player" that automatically switches between vertical and horizontal orientations depending on the device. Of course, the app is also integrating generative AI to allow for "conversational AI search."

🚨BREAKING FROM #BRAVOFANFEST🚨 Allow us to set the scene... Craig Conover stars in an all-new original scripted microdrama, Charmed by a Southern Monster! Stream November 9th on the @Peacock mobile app. #SouthernCharm pic.twitter.com/QpzjT0YE2X

Finally, NBCUniversal intends to continue investing in microdramas. These are extremely short (often just a minute long) serial dramas that connect to tell a larger story. The platform recently released something called Charmed by a Southern Monster, which is the company's first scripted microdrama. The new Peacock app is rolling out now via a phased release that could take until next year to reach every user.

It's worth noting that Peacock recently became profitable for the first time ever. As a matter of fact, it has experienced two straight quarters of profit. We'll have to wait and see if this app redesign helps to continue this trend.

#373. Cash App founder's killer gets 16 years to life in prison

Bob Lee died from multiple stab wounds in 2023.

Two years after his conviction for second-degree murder, Nima Momeni, the tech consultant found guilty of killing Cash App founder Bob Lee, has been sentenced. San Francisco Superior Court Judge Alexandra Gordon handed him to 16 years to life in prison.

Lee was instrumental in the development of Cash App while serving as chief technology officer at Square. He was also an engineer at Google from 2004 to 2010 and was involved in the development of Android. Lee was later the chief product officer at crypto payments company MobileCoin and helped the World Health Organization launch its mobile app during the COVID-19 pandemic.

In April 2023, Lee was found bleeding in a doorway on an empty downtown San Francisco street after being stabbed in the hip and heart. He managed to call 911 but died after being taken to a hospital.

Following the killing but before an arrest was made, some of Big Tech's loudest voices were quick to frame it in terms that supported their biases. "Many people I know have been severely assaulted" in San Francisco, Elon Musk tweeted, suggesting that "even if attackers are caught, they are often released immediately." Investor Jason Calacanis chimed in, calling those running the city "incompetent lunatics," while claiming the city was devolving into "utter chaos." (San Fransisco has a comparatively low crime rate, and has seen its homicide rate fall over the last four years.)

As it happened, this had nothing to do with governmental incompetence or random acts of violence. Lee was involved with Momeni's sister and had been partying with the two on the night of the murder. Prosecutors said Momeni killed Lee after learning that the Cash App founder had introduced his sister to drug dealers who abused her. Momeni's team claimed he acted in self-defense after Lee lunged at him with a knife. Lee was found to have cocaine, ketamine and alcohol in his system.

The judge rejected Momeni's request for a new trial. His lawyers say they plan to appeal.
